## Ezekiel 14:11 (id: 11497)

The goal of God’s judgment was not the total destruction of the exiles but their salvation, so that the people of Israel would learn not to remain faithful to the Lord.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 14:11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`)

## Ezekiel 14:12–20 (id: 11498)

* Israel had not been treated unfairly. God did not judge Israel without reason. If any nation sinned against the Lord in this way, that nation would face the same result.

    However, this passage clearly speaks about Israel. The words used to describe their sin also describe breaking the covenant (the binding promises between God and his people). Israel had broken this covenant. Because of that, the nation would rightly experience the covenant curses (the punishments promised for disobedience) listed in [Leviticus 26](https://ref.ly/Lev26:1-Lev26:46). The people had agreed to these terms when the covenant was first made.

* The covenant curses appear here in four test cases. Each case shows one of the judgments listed in [Leviticus 26](https://ref.ly/Lev26:1-Lev26:46):

    1. Famine (a severe lack of food), as described in [Ezekiel 14:13–14](https://ref.ly/Ezek14:13-Ezek14:14) and [Leviticus 26:26](https://ref.ly/Lev26:26).
        2. Wild animals attacking the land, as described in [Ezekiel 14:15–16](https://ref.ly/Ezek14:15-Ezek14:16) and [Leviticus 26:22](https://ref.ly/Lev26:22).
        3. War, as described in [Ezekiel 14:17–18](https://ref.ly/Ezek14:17-Ezek14:18) and [Leviticus 26:25](https://ref.ly/Lev26:25).
        4. Disease, as described in [Ezekiel 14:19–20](https://ref.ly/Ezek14:19-Ezek14:20) and [Leviticus 26:25](https://ref.ly/Lev26:25).
        These examples show that Israel’s suffering was not random. It was the direct result of breaking the covenant with the Lord.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 14:12-20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Job (ID: `person:Job`); Noah (ID: `person:Noah`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`); Be Unfaithful (ID: `keyterm:Unfaithful`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Hyena (ID: `fauna:Hyena`); Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`); Jackal (ID: `fauna:Jackal`); Bear (ID: `fauna:Bear`)

## Ezekiel 14:14 (id: 11499)

* *Noah, Daniel, and Job*: Each of these men was known for remaining faithful in a wicked generation. They lived among people who did evil, but they obeyed God.

    If anyone could deserve a delay in God’s judgment, it would be these men. However, even if a land included these three righteous men, their righteousness would not save their own sons or daughters from coming disaster ([Ezekiel 14:20](https://ref.ly/Ezek14:20)). Their obedience could only save themselves. It would certainly not save a rebellious nation.

* Some people question whether Ezekiel meant the prophet Daniel. In [Ezekiel 14](https://ref.ly/Ezek14:1-Ezek14:23) and [28:3](https://ref.ly/Ezek28:3), the Hebrew spelling of the name Daniel (*Dani’el*) is slightly different from the spelling in the book of Daniel (*Daniyye’l*). Because of this difference, some scholars suggest that Ezekiel referred to a legendary pagan hero named *Danel*.

    However, small differences in spelling are common in the Hebrew Old Testament. Ezekiel and his listeners would have known about the prophet Daniel, who lived in Babylon at the same time as Ezekiel. Daniel was already known as a model of righteousness and wisdom. It is unlikely that Ezekiel, who strongly opposed idolatry, would choose a pagan hero to represent perfect righteousness. For this reason, Ezekiel most likely meant the prophet Daniel.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 14:14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Job (ID: `person:Job`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Noah (ID: `person:Noah`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`)

## Ezekiel 14:21 (id: 11500)

Jerusalem was worse than the imaginary land described in [Ezekiel 14:12–20](https://ref.ly/Ezek14:12-Ezek14:20) in two ways. 

First, Jerusalem did not have men like Noah, Daniel, and Job. Instead, the city was full of unrighteous people. There were no well\-known righteous leaders whose faithfulness might stand out before God.

Second, Jerusalem would not suffer only one judgment. It would face all four terrible punishments at the same time. These included famine, wild animals, war, and disease, as described earlier in the chapter.

Because of this, it is not surprising that many people and animals in the city would die. The judgment would be complete and severe.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 14:21

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Hyena (ID: `fauna:Hyena`); Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`); Jackal (ID: `fauna:Jackal`); Bear (ID: `fauna:Bear`)

## Ezekiel 14:22–23 (id: 11501)

Some survivors would emerge from the devastating judgment ([Ezekiel 14:21](https://ref.ly/Ezek14:21)) and join those already in exile. They would not survive because of their own righteousness or that of their relatives, but as an object lesson for those in exile. As the exiles saw the depravity of this remnant, they would feel better about what God had done to Jerusalem. The exiles would understand that God had acted with justice in his judgment upon Jerusalem, not without cause.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 14:22-23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ezekiel 15:1–5 (id: 11507)

The wood of most trees can be used to make many useful objects. Even small pieces can serve as pegs, which are simple wooden pins used to hang or hold things in place.

The wood of a vine is different. It is weak and thin. It is not strong enough to make pegs or other tools.

It is not even useful as firewood. It burns too quickly and does not give much heat. In this way, vine wood is completely useless.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 15:1-5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Complete (ID: `keyterm:Complete`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Peg (ID: `realia:Peg.2`); Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`); Peg (ID: `realia:Peg`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Ezekiel 15:1–24.14 (id: 11506)

This section includes eight metaphors, each showing from a different angle the certainty of Jerusalem's upcoming judgment. The images are of: 

1. a worthless vine ([Ezekiel 15](https://ref.ly/Ezek15:1-Ezek15:8));
2. a faithless wife ([Ezekiel 16](https://ref.ly/Ezek16:1-Ezek16:63));
3. a vine and two eagles ([Ezekiel 17](https://ref.ly/Ezek17:1-Ezek17:24));
4. Sour grapes ([Ezekiel 18](https://ref.ly/Ezek18:1-Ezek18:32));
5. a lion and her cubs ([Ezekiel 19](https://ref.ly/Ezek19:1-Ezek19:14));
6. a sword ([Ezekiel 21](https://ref.ly/Ezek21:1-Ezek21:32));
7. two corrupt sisters ([Ezekiel 23](https://ref.ly/Ezek23:1-Ezek23:49)); and
8. a cooking pot ([Ezekiel 24](https://ref.ly/Ezek24:1-Ezek24:27)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 15:1-19:14

## Ezekiel 15:7 (id: 11509)

Anyone who escaped one “fire” of God’s judgment would not truly be safe. Here, “fire” is a picture of severe judgment.

This first fire likely refers to the defeat of Judah by King Nebuchadnezzar of Babylon in 597 BC ([2 Kings 21:1–4](https://ref.ly/2Kgs21:1-2Kgs21:4)). Some people survived that attack and were taken into exile.

But those who escaped that disaster would soon face another one. This second fire refers to the final destruction of Jerusalem in 586 BC. In other words, escaping the first judgment would not prevent the second.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 15:7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ezekiel 15:8 (id: 11510)

*unfaithfully*: See [Ezekiel 6:9](https://ref.ly/Ezek6:9); [16:17](https://ref.ly/Ezek16:17); [Hosea 2](https://ref.ly/Hos2:1-Hos2:23). Jerusalem had turned away from the Lord and followed idols instead of serving the true God. This was spiritual unfaithfulness. The prophets often describe this sin as breaking faith in a marriage relationship.

By worshiping idols, the people broke the covenant (the binding promises between God and his people). Because they broke this covenant, judgment would follow.

As a result, the land would become desolate (empty and ruined).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 15:8

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Be Unfaithful (ID: `keyterm:Unfaithful`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ezekiel 16:1–3 (id: 11511)

Ezekiel begins by describing Jerusalem’s weak and unlikely beginnings. He says the city came from Canaanite roots and was like the child of an Amorite father and a Hittite mother. 

The city existed long before Israel entered the land under Joshua. During Joshua’s campaign, Israel did not fully capture Jerusalem. The city remained in the hands of its original Canaanite population.

Later, King David captured Jerusalem and made it his capital. Even then, the city had a long Canaanite history before it became the center of Israel’s kingdom.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:1-3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Canaanites (ID: `group:Canaan`); Hittites (ID: `group:Hittite`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Heth (ID: `person:Heth`); Amorites (ID: `group:Amorites`)

## Ezekiel 16:1–63 (id: 11512)

In [Ezekiel 16](https://ref.ly/Ezek16:1-Ezek16:63), Jerusalem is described as an unfaithful wife and a prostitute. This language is very strong. Even in English, the chapter can be difficult to read. In its ancient setting, it would have sounded even more shocking.

Ezekiel used graphic language to show how serious Judah’s sin was. He did not try to make his message softer or more polite. He spoke in a direct and forceful way.

This strong language is the main point of the chapter. Ezekiel wanted the people to feel how terrible their sin was in God’s sight. The shocking description helped them understand that God’s judgment was not unfair.

If Ezekiel had used softer words, the people might not have understood the full seriousness of their sin. The strong picture was necessary to show why God’s judgment was justified (that is, right and fair).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:1-63

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Fornication (ID: `keyterm:Fornication`); Play the Prostitute (ID: `keyterm:PlayTheProstitute`); Sodom (ID: `place:Sodom`); Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`); Samaria (ID: `place:Samaria`); High Place (ID: `realia:HighPlace`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Anointing Oil (ID: `realia:AnointingOil`); Olive Oil (ID: `realia:OliveOil`); Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`); Hittites (ID: `group:Hittite`); Heth (ID: `person:Heth`); Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Amorites (ID: `group:Amorites`); Embroidered Cloth (ID: `realia:EmbroideredCloth`); Silk (ID: `realia:Silk`); Assyria (ID: `place:Assyria`); Decree (ID: `keyterm:Decree`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Bear the Consequences Of (ID: `keyterm:BearTheConsequencesOf`); Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe`); Canaanites (ID: `group:Canaan`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`)

## Ezekiel 16:4–5 (id: 11520)

Jerusalem’s parents are described as cruel and uncaring. They did not care for her when she was born. They did not do the normal things people did to help a newborn baby.

Usually, someone cut the umbilical cord. They washed the baby. They rubbed salt and oil on the child’s skin to clean and protect her. Then they wrapped the baby tightly in cloth to keep her warm and safe.

But none of this happened to Jerusalem. Instead, she was thrown into an open field and left there. In the ancient world, people often abandoned baby girls in this way. She was left alone to die.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:4-5


## Ezekiel 16:6–7 (id: 11526)

Jerusalem was helpless and close to death. She had no one to protect her. At that moment, the Lord stepped in and spoke a life\-giving word. Without his word, she would have died.

The Lord did not owe this abandoned child anything. Many other children in the ancient world were left to die. He was not required to rescue her. But because of his grace and mercy (his undeserved kindness and compassion), he chose to save her.

The Lord did more than keep her alive. He helped her grow strong. Instead of dying in the field, she grew like a healthy plant. She became mature and beautiful.

The city of Jerusalem became strong and successful even before it was an Israelite city. This growth did not happen by accident. It was the Lord’s work.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:6-7


## Ezekiel 16:8 (id: 11527)

At this point, the Lord covered her with his cloak. In the ancient world, this action showed a promise of marriage (compare [Ruth 3:9](https://ref.ly/Ruth3:9)). It was a sign that he was taking her as his own.

The Lord then made a covenant with Jerusalem. In the picture Ezekiel uses, this covenant is like a marriage. The Lord chose her and joined himself to her.

Later, the Lord made a covenant with David and his descendants. At that time, he also chose Jerusalem as the place where his name would be honored (see [1 Kings 9:3–4](https://ref.ly/1Kgs9:3-1Kgs9:4); [Psalm 132](https://ref.ly/Ps132:1-Ps132:18)). Jerusalem became the special city connected to his presence and worship.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:8

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Swear (ID: `keyterm:Swear`); Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`)

## Ezekiel 16:9–10 (id: 11528)

* The Lord did for Jerusalem what her parents never did: he washed, anointed, and clothed her, reversing the circumstances of her birth.
* The Lord gave Jerusalem beautiful gifts. He dressed her with ornaments and fine materials fit for a queen. Some of these same materials were used in the tabernacle (see [Exodus 25:3–5](https://ref.ly/Exod25:3-Exod25:5); [26:1–14](https://ref.ly/Exod26:1-Exod26:14)).

    This detail would remind the people of Jerusalem of something important. Their city was chosen as the place for God’s sanctuary and the king’s palace. The Lord honored Jerusalem by making her the center of worship and royal rule.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:9-10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Silk (ID: `realia:Silk`); Embroidered Cloth (ID: `realia:EmbroideredCloth`); Linen (ID: `realia:Linen`); Olive Oil (ID: `realia:OliveOil`); Dugong (ID: `fauna:Dugong`); Anointing Oil (ID: `realia:AnointingOil`)

## Ezekiel 16:11–14 (id: 11513)

She wore jewelry and ate the finest foods. She was known throughout the world for her beauty and splendor, which were gifts from the Lord.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:11-14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Silk (ID: `realia:Silk`); Crown (ID: `realia:Crown`); Embroidered Cloth (ID: `realia:EmbroideredCloth`); Linen (ID: `realia:Linen`); Olive Oil (ID: `realia:OliveOil`); Earring (ID: `realia:Earring`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Anointing Oil (ID: `realia:AnointingOil`)

## Ezekiel 16:15–19 (id: 11514)

Jerusalem did not appreciate the good things God gave her. She used her fame and beauty for false gods. She offered clothes, jewels, food, and oil to idols instead of honoring God.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:15-19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Play the Prostitute (ID: `keyterm:PlayTheProstitute`); Olive Oil (ID: `realia:OliveOil`); Anointing Oil (ID: `realia:AnointingOil`); Trust (ID: `keyterm:LeanOn`); Pleasing Odor (ID: `keyterm:PleasingOdor`); Fornication (ID: `keyterm:Fornication`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Stone Pine (ID: `flora:Pine`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`); Embroidered Cloth (ID: `realia:EmbroideredCloth`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); High Place (ID: `realia:HighPlace`)

## Ezekiel 16:20–22 (id: 11515)

Jerusalem even sacrificed her sons and daughters to false gods. The nations around Israel practiced child sacrifice to show total commitment to a deity. This was common in the worship of the gods Molech and Chemosh (see [Deuteronomy 12:31](https://ref.ly/Deut12:31); [2 Kings 3:27](https://ref.ly/2Kgs3:27)). Israel sometimes took part in this terrible sin.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:20-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fornication (ID: `keyterm:Fornication`)

## Ezekiel 16:23–25 (id: 11516)

The prophets often described idolatry (worshiping false gods) as adultery (being unfaithful in marriage). For example, Hosea uses this picture to explain Israel’s sin (see [Hosea 2](https://ref.ly/Hos2:1-Hos2:23)). This comparison helped people understand that turning away from the Lord was like breaking a marriage promise.

Ezekiel, however, gives much more detail than the other prophets. He does not describe Jerusalem as only confused or mistaken. He describes her as deeply corrupt. Her unfaithfulness took place openly, on every street corner.

Ezekiel also says that her desire for sin had no limit. She kept seeking more sinful and shameful practices. His strong language shows how serious her idolatry had become.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:23-25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** High Place (ID: `realia:HighPlace`); Fornication (ID: `keyterm:Fornication`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe`)

## Ezekiel 16:26–29 (id: 11517)

Jerusalem eagerly sought close relationships with Egypt, Assyria, and Babylonia. These political alliances cost a great deal of money. They also failed to bring the help and protection that the leaders expected.

Even if these agreements had brought clear political benefits, they would still have been wrong. They showed that Jerusalem did not trust the Lord. Instead of depending on him, the city looked to other nations for safety.

These alliances also led to another serious problem. Israel began to worship the gods of those nations. Political dependence slowly became spiritual unfaithfulness.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:26-29

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Play the Prostitute (ID: `keyterm:PlayTheProstitute`); Fornication (ID: `keyterm:Fornication`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`); Assyria (ID: `place:Assyria`); Decree (ID: `keyterm:Decree`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`); Chaldea (ID: `place:Chaldea`)

## Ezekiel 16:35–38 (id: 11518)

Because Jerusalem acted like an unfaithful spouse, it was appropriate for her to face a similar punishment. God would symbolically strip her of her dignity, reversing the marriage metaphor of being clothed (see [Ezekiel 16:8](https://ref.ly/Ezek16:8); [Hosea 2:2–3](https://ref.ly/Hos2:2-Hos2:3)). 

Then the people would stone her ([Leviticus 20:10](https://ref.ly/Lev20:10); [Deuteronomy 22:22](https://ref.ly/Deut22:22)). Since this would cover her naked body with blood*,* she would leave the world just as she came into it. This metaphor was fulfilled when the Babylonians destroyed the city in 586 BC.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:35-38

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Fornication (ID: `keyterm:Fornication`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`)

## Ezekiel 16:39–43 (id: 11519)

In a tragic turn, the nations that Jerusalem trusted would later attack her. The very allies she depended on would turn against her and destroy her.

The Lord’s anger would continue until justice was complete. It would not end until Jerusalem had paid the full price for her past sins. Only then would his judgment be finished.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:39-43

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`); High Place (ID: `realia:HighPlace`)

## Ezekiel 16:44–45 (id: 11521)

Jerusalem’s “parents” were a Hittite and an Amorite. Hittites and Amorites were previous occupants of Canaan who were cut off from the land because of their sins (compare [Genesis 15:16](https://ref.ly/Gen15:16)). The pagan city of Jerusalem was captured and became part of Israel during David's time ([2 Samuel 5:6–10](https://ref.ly/2Sam5:6-2Sam5:10)). Jerusalem’s later actions matched her family background. She behaved in a way that fit her heredity (family line and ancestry).

In other words, her conduct was consistent with where she came from. Her actions reflected the character of her origins.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:44-45

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Heth (ID: `person:Heth`); Hittites (ID: `group:Hittite`); Amorites (ID: `group:Amorites`)

## Ezekiel 16:46–50 (id: 11522)

* Samaria, Jerusalem’s older (or *larger*) sister, engaged in improper worship since Jeroboam introduced golden calves at the national shrines in Dan and Bethel ([1 Kings 12:28–33](https://ref.ly/1Kgs12:28-1Kgs12:33)).
* Sodom, Jerusalem’s younger (or *smaller*) sister, became known for sexual sin ([Genesis 19:4–9](https://ref.ly/Gen19:4-Gen19:9)) and for pride, gluttony, laziness, and neglect of the poor and needy.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:46-50

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sodom (ID: `place:Sodom`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Samaria (ID: `place:Samaria`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`)

Compared to Jerusalem, Samaria and Sodom seemed good. If God rightly destroyed both of Jerusalem's sisters for their sins, how could Jerusalem avoid God's coming anger?

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:51-52

God's grace, more than his judgment, would make Jerusalem ashamed of her ties to such "parents" and "sisters" ([Ezekiel 16:44–52](https://ref.ly/Ezek16:44-Ezek16:52)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:53-54

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sodom (ID: `place:Sodom`); Samaria (ID: `place:Samaria`)

## Ezekiel 16:59–63 (id: 11525)

Jerusalem's sins were serious and required judgment. But judgment was not God's final word on Jerusalem. She had broken God's covenant in many ways. Because of this, she deserved the penalty of death. 

But judgment was not God’s final word. The Lord would remember the covenant he made with her at the beginning. His plans for his people could not be stopped, even by their sin. His covenant commitment lasts forever ([Psalm 136](https://ref.ly/Ps136:1-Ps136:26)). 

In the end, God would forgive her sins. His forgiveness would lead Jerusalem to repentance.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 16:59-63

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`); Oath (ID: `keyterm:Oath`)

## Ezekiel 17:1–24 (id: 11529)

This chapter includes a riddle. A riddle is a type of metaphorical speech (language that uses pictures and comparisons). It both hides and shows the meaning at the same time.

The chapter is also a fable. A fable is a story that teaches a moral lesson. It often tells the lesson through plants or animals instead of people.

This creative setting creates distance between the story and real life. That distance helps the listener receive the message. It lowers resistance to a message that may be hard or unpleasant to hear.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 17:1-24

## Ezekiel 17:7–9 (id: 11535)

* There was a second great eagle. It was like the first eagle, but it was not as impressive or powerful.
* The future of the vine was easy to foresee. In trying to gain more, it would lose what it already had. Its desire for more would bring loss instead of gain.

    The second eagle would not help the vine. At the same time, the first eagle would become angry. That anger would be justified because the vine had been unfaithful.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 17:7-9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Vulture (ID: `fauna:Vulture`)

## Ezekiel 17:10 (id: 11530)

In Judah, the east wind blows from the desert and is therefore hot and dry.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 17:10


## Ezekiel 17:11–18 (id: 11531)

* The first eagle represents Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon. The cedar sprig represents Jehoiachin, whom Nebuchadnezzar took away to Babylon. The plant that later grew into a low vine represents Zedekiah. The second eagle represents Egypt. Zedekiah asked Egypt for help because he wanted to break free from Babylon’s rule.

    The hot east wind represents judgment from Babylon. This wind pulled up and dried out Jerusalem. In other words, Babylon destroyed the city as an act of judgment.

* The picture of the eagle caring for the vine may describe God’s care for Israel more than Nebuchadnezzar’s concern for Zedekiah. This comparison shows an important lesson. Zedekiah rebelled against his overlord (the king who ruled over him), Nebuchadnezzar. In a similar way, Israel rebelled against the Lord.

    Zedekiah’s rebellion against Babylon was extremely foolish. It led to destruction and death. Israel’s rebellion against the Lord, the God of heaven and earth, was even more foolish.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 17:11-18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`); Oath (ID: `keyterm:Oath`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Leader (ID: `keyterm:Leader`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Hophra (ID: `person:Hophra`); Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`); Siege-Works (ID: `realia:Siege-works`); Siege Ramp (ID: `realia:SiegeRamp`)

## Ezekiel 17:19–21 (id: 11532)

* God would punish Israel’s king because he broke his covenant. By turning against Babylon, he also committed treason (betrayal against a ruler). In doing this, he sinned against the Lord, who had placed him in the promised land.
* *will bring him to Babylon and execute judgment upon him*: See [2 Kings 25:5–7](https://ref.ly/2Kgs25:5-2Kgs25:7).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 17:19-21

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Be Unfaithful (ID: `keyterm:Unfaithful`); Oath (ID: `keyterm:Oath`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`); Fishnet (ID: `realia:Fishnet`); Net (ID: `realia:Net`); Casting Net (ID: `realia:CastingNet`)

## Ezekiel 17:22–24 (id: 11533)

* The last part of the chapter changes the story. Now the Lord takes a branch from the cedar tree and plants it on Israel’s highest mountain. In the Old Testament, a tree often represents a royal family line. A new shoot (a small new branch) represents a new beginning (compare [Isaiah 11:1](https://ref.ly/Isa11:1)).

    The judgment on the vine would not end the monarchy (the rule of kings). God would plant a new branch. This branch would grow into a greater cedar than the first one. Although the line of kings seemed to end with Zedekiah, this was not the final outcome.

    In God’s time, a new beginning was certain (see [Haggai 2:21–23](https://ref.ly/Hag2:21-Hag2:23); [Matthew 1:11–16](https://ref.ly/Matt1:11-Matt1:16); [2:1–11](https://ref.ly/Matt2:1-Matt2:11)).

* God cuts down the tall tree, makes the short tree grow tall, and revives the dead tree, allowing birds of every kind to find shelter and shade under its branches. In this picture, birds of every kind represent the nations.

    God had promised David an eternal throne (a kingdom that would not end). The failures of David’s descendants, the kings of Judah, could not cancel that promise. One day, the family line of David would rise again. This happened through Jesus, who brings blessing to all nations.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 17:22-24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Cedar (ID: `flora:Cedar`); Mighty (ID: `keyterm:Mighty`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Bird (ID: `fauna:Bird`)

## Ezekiel 18:1–2 (id: 11536)

*The fathers have eaten sour grapes, and the teeth of the children are set on edge:* The people were repeating a proverb. This meant that innocent children sometimes suffer for their parents’ actions. 

In Ezekiel’s time, the people used this saying to make a complaint. They believed that their ancestors had committed the sins that caused the exile. They thought they were suffering for sins they did not commit (compare [Lamentations 5:7](https://ref.ly/Lam5:7)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 18:1-2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Proverb (ID: `keyterm:Saying`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

The Lord's response to the proverb of [Ezekiel 18:1–2](https://ref.ly/Ezek18:1-Ezek18:2) was to clearly state that it did not apply to this situation. Instead, the Lord always punishes only the guilty ([Deuteronomy 24:16](https://ref.ly/Deut24:16)). God is always fair and just.

The righteous son of an evil man will surely live. God will judge each person individually.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 18:14-18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`)

## Ezekiel 18:21–24 (id: 11539)

Ezekiel gives two more examples.

First, wicked people (those who do evil) who turn away from their sins can receive God’s forgiveness.

Second, righteous people (those who live in a right way before God) who begin to sin will face judgment.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 18:21-24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Be Unfaithful (ID: `keyterm:Unfaithful`)

## Ezekiel 18:23–24 (id: 11540)

God does not want wicked people to die. He made Ezekiel a watchman to guide the wicked toward a godly life and to warn the righteous not to stray ([Ezekiel 3:16–19](https://ref.ly/Ezek3:16-Ezek3:19); [33:1–9](https://ref.ly/Ezek33:1-Ezek33:9)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 18:23-24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Be Unfaithful (ID: `keyterm:Unfaithful`)

## Ezekiel 18:25–29 (id: 11541)

Israel's issue was not that the Lord was unjust, but that they kept doing wrong. They fully deserved God's judgment.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 18:25-29

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); The Way (ID: `keyterm:TheWay`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`)

## Ezekiel 18:30–32 (id: 11543)

This chapter ends with a strong appeal to the people of Israel. God calls them to turn back to him and live.

It was not too late for them to repent. They could turn from their sins and receive forgiveness. God promised to give them a new heart and a new spirit ([Ezekiel 11:19](https://ref.ly/Ezek11:19); [36:26](https://ref.ly/Ezek36:26)).

A new heart and a new spirit mean an inner change. God would change their thoughts and desires. He would do this for all who turned from their rebellion and came to him with humility.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 18:30-32

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`)

## Ezekiel 19:1–14 (id: 11548)

* In the ancient Near East, a funeral song had a special rhythm and style. It usually praised the good qualities of the person who had died. It also compared past glory with the present loss.

    In this chapter, however, the people being mourned were not yet dead. The song does not praise them. Instead, it lists their sins and failures. This funeral song clearly shows that their judgment was certain and explains why it would happen.

* The lion ([Ezekiel 19:2–9](https://ref.ly/Ezek19:2-Ezek19:9)) and the vine ([19:10–14](https://ref.ly/Ezek19:10-Ezek19:14)) were common pictures for Israel’s princes and for the royal family of Judah. These images would have been familiar to the people who first heard this message.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 19:1-14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`); Lamentation (ID: `keyterm:Lamentation`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Harpoon (ID: `realia:Harpoon`); Rod (ID: `realia:Rod`); Staff (ID: `realia:Staff`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); Hope (ID: `keyterm:Hope`); Cage (ID: `realia:Cage`); Net (ID: `realia:Net`); Date Palm (ID: `flora:DatePalm`); Fortress (ID: `realia:Fortress`); Casting Net (ID: `realia:CastingNet`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Ezekiel 19:2–4 (id: 11552)

* The first picture shows a lioness and one of her cubs, whom she chose as the leader of her group. This cub represents Jehoahaz, who ruled for only three months before Pharaoh Neco took him to Egypt ([2 Kings 23:33–34](https://ref.ly/2Kgs23:33-2Kgs23:34)).
* *tear his prey … devoured men*: The prophet describes Jehoahaz’s short reign in completely negative terms. He presents his rule as evil and harmful.
* In ancient times, hunters often caught lions with a net and a pit. In this passage, that picture is a metaphor for what would happen to Jehoahaz. He would be captured and taken away to Egypt by force.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 19:2-4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Harpoon (ID: `realia:Harpoon`)

## Ezekiel 19:5–7 (id: 11553)

The second cub acted like the first one, but he was even more violent. He destroyed towns and cities.

This cub may represent Jehoiakim, Jehoiachin, or Zedekiah. The text does not clearly say which one.

It is also not clear which towns and cities were destroyed. The prophet may be describing the harmful results of the king’s foolish foreign policy (his decisions about relationships with other nations). These choices brought suffering to the towns and cities of Judah.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 19:5-7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`); Hope (ID: `keyterm:Hope`)

## Ezekiel 19:8–9 (id: 11554)

The Babylonians captured and killed Jehoiakim in Judah. They exiled Jehoiachin and Ezekiel to Babylon. Zedekiah's rule ended when Jerusalem was destroyed in 586 BC.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 19:8-9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cage (ID: `realia:Cage`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); Harpoon (ID: `realia:Harpoon`); Net (ID: `realia:Net`); Fortress (ID: `realia:Fortress`); Casting Net (ID: `realia:CastingNet`)

## Ezekiel 19:10 (id: 11549)

The vine clearly represents Judah, whom the Lord had planted and cared for in the best possible conditions.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 19:10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Date Palm (ID: `flora:DatePalm`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Ezekiel 19:11–12 (id: 11550)

Judah’s pride led to its fall. The Lord removed the nation in his anger (compare [Ezekiel 17:1–10](https://ref.ly/Ezek17:1-Ezek17:10)). He then placed Judah in exile. Exile is life away from the land God had given them. It was like being planted in a dry desert.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 19:11-12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rod (ID: `realia:Rod`); Staff (ID: `realia:Staff`)

## Ezekiel 19:13–14 (id: 11551)

The fire that came from the vine’s own branches probably represents Zedekiah. That fire burned up the fruit. The fruit represents the land and the people.

After this destruction, no strong branch remained. There was no branch able to become a ruler’s scepter (a symbol of kingly authority). Zedekiah would not have an immediate successor.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 19:13-14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lamentation (ID: `keyterm:Lamentation`); Rod (ID: `realia:Rod`); Staff (ID: `realia:Staff`)

## Ezekiel 20:1–3 (id: 11563)

* In the seventh year of King Jehoiachin’s captivity, five more years remained before Jerusalem's destruction.
* The leaders (literally *elders*) of Israel, who were the community's elders in exile, came to Ezekiel again, seeking a message from the Lord (compare [Ezekiel 8:1](https://ref.ly/Ezek8:1); [14:1](https://ref.ly/Ezek14:1)).

    Normally, it is good to seek a message from the Lord. But these leaders had already been judged because their motives were divided (see [Ezekiel 14](https://ref.ly/Ezek14:1-Ezek14:23)). They did not truly seek God with sincere hearts.

    Because of this, the Lord refused to answer them. The text does not record the question they planned to ask Ezekiel. It is possible they never had the chance to ask it.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 20:1-3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`)

## Ezekiel 20:4–26 (id: 11570)

The Lord refused to answer their question. But this did not mean he had nothing to say.

Instead, God gave them a different message through Ezekiel. Ezekiel would describe the sinful and detestable actions of their ancestors. He would place these past sins clearly before the people.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 20:4-26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`); To Make an Oath (ID: `keyterm:LiftUp`); Bear the Consequences Of (ID: `keyterm:BearTheConsequencesOf`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Detested Thing (ID: `keyterm:DetestedThing`); Decree (ID: `keyterm:Decree`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Land Flowing of Milk and Honey (ID: `keyterm:LandFlowingOfMilkAndHoney`)

## Ezekiel 20:8–21 (id: 11574)

Each generation of Israelites rebelled against the Lord. They refused to obey the commandments he gave them.

Each time, the Lord warned that he would pour out his fury (his strong anger) on them ([Ezekiel 20:8](https://ref.ly/Ezek20:8), [13](https://ref.ly/Ezek20:13), [21](https://ref.ly/Ezek20:21)). But he held back his judgment. He did this for the honor of his name. 

If he had destroyed them, the surrounding nations might have thought he was too weak to bring his people into the promised land.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 20:8-21

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`); Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); To Make an Oath (ID: `keyterm:LiftUp`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Detested Thing (ID: `keyterm:DetestedThing`); Decree (ID: `keyterm:Decree`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Bear the Consequences Of (ID: `keyterm:BearTheConsequencesOf`); Land Flowing of Milk and Honey (ID: `keyterm:LandFlowingOfMilkAndHoney`)

## Ezekiel 20:23 (id: 11564)

Israel had a long history of refusing to obey the Lord’s decrees and regulations. Because of this, God decided to scatter them among the nations. He sent them away from their land and spread them among many different peoples.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 20:23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Make an Oath (ID: `keyterm:LiftUp`); Bear the Consequences Of (ID: `keyterm:BearTheConsequencesOf`)

## Ezekiel 20:25–26 (id: 11565)

*I also gave them over to statutes that were not good and ordinances … I pronounced them unclean*: This means that the Lord allowed the people of Israel to follow their own sinful desires. He did not stop them from choosing false worship and harmful practices.

They took part in the complex and corrupt rituals of paganism (the worship of false gods). As a result, they suffered the painful consequences of their sins (see [Romans 1:18–25](https://ref.ly/Rom1:18-Rom1:25)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 20:25-26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Decree (ID: `keyterm:Decree`)

## Ezekiel 20:26 (id: 11566)

The Israelites even offered their firstborn children as sacrifices to the god Molech. This was a terrible sin.

This action reversed what God had done in the exodus. At that time, the Lord called Israel his “firstborn son” ([Exodus 4:22](https://ref.ly/Exod4:22)). He rescued them from slavery so they could worship him in purity in the promised land.

Instead of honoring the Lord, they gave their children to a false god. Their actions showed how far they had turned away from him.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 20:26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`)

## Ezekiel 20:27–31 (id: 11567)

When they reached the promised land, the people of Israel continued to speak against and betray the Lord. Their idol worship and wickedness persisted until Ezekiel's time. The Lord would not answer such unfaithful people.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 20:27-31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`); Be Unfaithful (ID: `keyterm:Unfaithful`); To Make an Oath (ID: `keyterm:LiftUp`); Play the Prostitute (ID: `keyterm:PlayTheProstitute`); Detested Thing (ID: `keyterm:DetestedThing`); Pleasing Odor (ID: `keyterm:PleasingOdor`); Gibeon (ID: `place:Gibeon`); Slaughtering (ID: `keyterm:Slaughtering`); Bear the Consequences Of (ID: `keyterm:BearTheConsequencesOf`); Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); Stone Pine (ID: `flora:Pine`); Wine (ID: `realia:Wine`); High Place (ID: `realia:HighPlace`)

## Ezekiel 20:32–38 (id: 11568)

* In the past, Israel rebelled against God. Because of this, God judged them in limited ways. As a result, they were scattered among other nations again.

    However, judgment was not the end of the story. Earlier events in Israel’s history showed that God does not forget his promises. God’s own honor required him to keep his covenant with his people, even when they sinned.

* Israel could never become like the surrounding nations who worship idols made of wood and stone ([Ezekiel 20:32](https://ref.ly/Ezek20:32)). God had chosen Israel to belong to him. Because of this, he would gather them again.

    God said he would bring them into the wilderness, as he did in the first exodus from Egypt. This would be like a new exodus. But this was not only good news.

    After the first exodus, an entire generation died in the wilderness because of their sin. In the same way, God would judge this new generation. He would remove those who continued to rebel and refuse to obey him.

    Those rebels would not enter the land of Israel. The wilderness among the nations would become their final resting place.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 20:32-38

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`); Rod (ID: `realia:Rod`); Staff (ID: `realia:Staff`)

## Ezekiel 20:39–44 (id: 11569)

The people of Israel might continue to worship idols for a time. However, in the end, they would worship God in spirit and in truth on his holy mountain (see [Ezekiel 40–48](https://ref.ly/Ezek40:1-Ezek48:35), where the purified worship of God is restored in the temple). Jesus also spoke about worshiping the Father in spirit and in truth ([John 4:21–24](https://ref.ly/John4:21-John4:24)). 

God chose Israel to be a holy nation. His purpose in choosing them would not fail. In the end, his plan would stand.

The people would become like a pleasing sacrifice to God. This does not mean they would be killed. It means their lives would fully belong to him. Through them, God’s holiness would be clearly seen.

This new exodus would lead to pure worship. A purified people would offer that worship. God would save them by his sovereign grace (his rule and undeserved kindness).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 20:39-44

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); To Make an Oath (ID: `keyterm:LiftUp`); Pleasing Odor (ID: `keyterm:PleasingOdor`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Bear the Consequences Of (ID: `keyterm:BearTheConsequencesOf`); To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`)

## Ezekiel 20:45–49 (id: 11571)

Like a parable, this prophecy both reveals and conceals its message. People complain that the prophet only speaks in riddles (see [Matthew 13:10–17](https://ref.ly/Matt13:10-Matt13:17)). It reveals that a powerful judgment is coming. This judgment is described as an all\-consuming fire. That means it is like a fire that burns up every tree.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 20:45-49

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Negev (ID: `place:Negeb`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`); Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); Proverb (ID: `keyterm:Saying`)

## Ezekiel 20:46 (id: 11572)

* *set your face toward the south* (literally *turn toward Teman*): Teman was a town in Edom, southeast of Judah.
* The Negev was southwest of the Dead Sea.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 20:46

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Negev (ID: `place:Negeb`); Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`)

## Ezekiel 20:47 (id: 11573)

A green tree usually does not burn easily, but a dry tree catches fire quickly. This judgment fire would be so intense that it would burn all types of trees.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 20:47

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Negev (ID: `place:Negeb`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`)

Each time, the sword represents God’s judgment.

## Ezekiel 21:3–5 (id: 11583)

* The Lord himself was the true enemy Israel needed to fear. He was about to bring a complete judgment against them.
* Normally, people expect the righteous to escape judgment and the wicked to receive it. This contrast is like the green tree and the dry tree in the parable ([Ezekiel 20:47–48](https://ref.ly/Ezek20:47-Ezek20:48)).

    God’s judgment on sin would be like a very hot fire. It would burn everything it touched.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 21:3-5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sheath (ID: `realia:Sheath`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Ezekiel 21:6–7 (id: 11585)

Ezekiel's groaning showed that in the coming judgment, even the bravest would feel fear and the strong would become weak. The judgment that had been announced was now a harsh reality (compare [Ezekiel 7:1–4](https://ref.ly/Ezek7:1-Ezek7:4)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 21:6-7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ezekiel 21:8–11 (id: 11586)

*sword, sharpened and polished*: These actions prepare the weapon to be deadly and effective.

Once the sword is ready, it will be given to the executioner (the one who carries out a death sentence). He will use it against God’s people.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 21:8-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`); Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`)

## Ezekiel 21:12 (id: 11576)

*Cry out and wail*: Ezekiel would represent the people’s response to the judgment.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 21:12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`)

## Ezekiel 21:14–17 (id: 11577)

As the Lord's representative, Ezekiel was to clap his hands in a threatening way and wave the sword three times. This action symbolized the complete massacre to come. 

There would be no place to escape. No one could hide from the sword of judgment. When the Lord was ready to act, he would destroy his people. In this way, he would satisfy his fury (his burning anger). They would be filled with terror. Their courage would disappear.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 21:14-17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`)

## Ezekiel 21:18–20 (id: 11578)

The sword of the Lord was not just a symbol. It would become the sword of the king of Babylon.

That king was Nebuchadnezzar. His army would carry out God’s judgment.

Ezekiel showed this in a visual way. He drew a map to show the king’s two possible targets. One was Rabbah, the capital of Ammon. The other was Jerusalem, the capital of Judah.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 21:18-20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`); Ammon (ID: `place:Ammon`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Ammonites (ID: `group:Ammon`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ezekiel 21:21 (id: 11579)

* Omens were signs that people believed came from the gods. People looked for these signs through divination (trying to learn the future through special rituals).
* *He shakes the arrows, he consults the idols, he examines the liver*: These were common ways people tried to receive omens from their gods.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 21:21

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); Teraphim (ID: `deity:Teraphim`); Practice Divination (ID: `keyterm:PracticeDivination`); Fee for Divination (ID: `keyterm:FeeForDivination`); Arrow (ID: `realia:Arrow`); Household Idols (ID: `realia:HouseholdIdols`)

## Ezekiel 21:23–24 (id: 11580)

A treaty with the Babylonians would not save the people of Jerusalem because they had broken its terms. The king of Babylon would remind them of their rebellion. He would show in public that rebelling against a covenant overlord (a powerful ruler who made a binding agreement) brings serious consequences. If rebellion against their Babylonian ruler brought punishment, how much more would rebellion against the Lord bring judgment?

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 21:23-24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Practice Divination (ID: `keyterm:PracticeDivination`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ezekiel 21:25–27 (id: 11581)

* This judgment would affect Zedekiah, the corrupt and wicked leader of Israel, and the people. Ezekiel refers to Zedekiah by his title, showing that his position was also judged. He would lose his royal symbols and be humbled, while the Lord would lift up the humble. The old order would face destruction.
* *the arrival of Him … to whom I have assigned the right of judgment*: Many people understand this coming judge to be the Messiah (God’s chosen one), as in [Genesis 49:10](https://ref.ly/Gen49:10). In this context, however, the Lord was giving Judah to the Babylonians for judgment (see [Ezekiel 23:24](https://ref.ly/Ezek23:24)).

    Ezekiel was likely reshaping the traditional promise in [Genesis 49:10](https://ref.ly/Gen49:10). Instead of pointing to a future king, he applied it to a coming judgment. Nebuchadnezzar would act as God’s instrument. God would take the scepter (symbol of royal rule) away from Judah for a time because its rulers had sinned. Later, God would restore it.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 21:25-27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Crown (ID: `realia:Crown`); Turban (ID: `realia:Turban`)

## Ezekiel 21:28–29 (id: 11582)

The Ammonites should not have celebrated when Nebuchadnezzar chose to go to Jerusalem instead of Rabbah ([Ezekiel 21:18–20](https://ref.ly/Ezek21:18-Ezek21:20)). They were also among the wicked facing judgment day.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 21:28-29

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); Have a Vision (ID: `keyterm:HaveAVision`); Ammon (ID: `place:Ammon`); Practice Divination (ID: `keyterm:PracticeDivination`); Ammonites (ID: `group:Ammon`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`)

## Ezekiel 21:30–32 (id: 11584)

The sword would return to its sheath. This does not mean it would rest. It would return in order to carry out the Lord’s judgment against its own land, Babylon.

God would also pour out his fury and the fire of his anger on Babylon. These words describe God’s intense judgment. Babylon did not receive special protection just because God had used it to judge other nations.

Judgment may have begun with God’s household ([1 Peter 4:17](https://ref.ly/1Pet4:17)), but it did not stop there. God’s judgment also included the surrounding nations that did not worship him.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 21:30-32

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sheath (ID: `realia:Sheath`)

## Ezekiel 22:1–5 (id: 11588)

Now God called Ezekiel to speak like a prosecutor in a court. He announced the charges against Jerusalem that would lead to its judgment.

The people were guilty of sins against other people. They had shed innocent blood (see [Genesis 9:5–6](https://ref.ly/Gen9:5-Gen9:6)). They were also guilty of sins against God. They made idols, which God had forbidden (see [Exodus 20:4–6](https://ref.ly/Exod20:4-Exod20:6)). 

These two kinds of sin made the city unclean. They made Jerusalem guilty and deserving of judgment. The people were no longer fit to stand before the holy God. As a result, Jerusalem faced certain destruction and shame.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 22:1-5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`)

## Ezekiel 22:1–31 (id: 11587)

Jerusalem was the holy city where God had placed his name. It was the spiritual center of Judah. The city represented God’s presence among his people.

But Jerusalem had become corrupt and unclean. Instead of honoring God, the city was full of violence and bloodshed. Because of this sin, God’s wrath (his just anger against sin) would come upon the city.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 22:1-31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`); Kiln (ID: `realia:Kiln`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); Pure (ID: `keyterm:Pure`); Profane (ID: `keyterm:Profane`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`); Instruction (ID: `keyterm:Instruction`); Practice Divination (ID: `keyterm:PracticeDivination`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Have a Vision (ID: `keyterm:HaveAVision`); Complete (ID: `keyterm:Complete`); Wolf (ID: `fauna:Wolf`); Plaster (ID: `realia:Plaster`); Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`)

## Ezekiel 22:6–12 (id: 11596)

The charges against the people of Judah and their leaders came from God's law, especially from [Leviticus 18–20](https://ref.ly/Lev18:1-Lev20:27); [25:1–55](https://ref.ly/Lev25:1-Lev25:55). Israel's many sins showed a failure to honor and trust the Lord and his commands. This unfaithfulness to their covenant with the Lord could lead to only one outcome: The people of Israel would face the covenant curses ([Deuteronomy 8:19–20](https://ref.ly/Deut8:19-Deut8:20); [28:15–68](https://ref.ly/Deut28:15-Deut28:68)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 22:6-12

## Ezekiel 22:13–16 (id: 11590)

God first showed his anger with the image of clapping his hands. This action expressed strong displeasure. Then he announced that he would pour out judgment.

The first judgment was exile. God would scatter the people of Judah among other nations. Through this scattering, God would remove their wickedness and cleanse them from sin.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 22:13-16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Complete (ID: `keyterm:Complete`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`)

## Ezekiel 22:16 (id: 11591)

*when you have defiled yourself in the eyes of the nations*: The Lord's people were scattered among the nations instead of living in the promised land. This situation made it seem like the Lord could not fulfill his promise. However, the Lord was willing to endure this dishonor so his forgetful people could learn to remember him.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 22:16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`)

## Ezekiel 22:17–22 (id: 11592)

Scattering was not the only part of God’s judgment. God would also gather the people of Judah into Jerusalem for judgment.

The prophet compared this gathering to metal placed into a furnace (a very hot oven used to melt metal). A worker gathers pieces of metal and puts them into the fire to melt and refine them.

But this fire would not make the people pure. Normally, a furnace removes waste material and leaves pure metal. In this case, the people were like worthless slag (waste left over after metal is melted). Since only waste would go into the fire, only melted waste would come out.

This judgment would not cleanse or restore the people. It would destroy everything in its path.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 22:17-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Kiln (ID: `realia:Kiln`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`)

## Ezekiel 22:25–29 (id: 11594)

* This list of Jerusalem’s sins focuses on the wrongdoings of Judah's leaders (compare [Zephaniah 3:3–4](https://ref.ly/Zeph3:3-Zeph3:4)).
* *princes*: The princes and other leaders abused their power by killing innocent people and taking their wealth. The priests sinned by failing to teach the law, preventing people from distinguishing between holy and unholy, clean and unclean. The prophets shared false visions instead of true messages from God. As a result, the people wandered away from God because they had no guidance.

## Ezekiel 22:30–31 (id: 11595)

Because of the sins of these former community leaders, the Lord looked for someone to rebuild the wall and act as a true prophet (see [Ezekiel 13:5](https://ref.ly/Ezek13:5)). This person would intercede for the people, like Moses did after they sinned with the golden calf ([Exodus 32](https://ref.ly/Exod32:1-Exod32:35)). The Lord found no one to turn away his wrath. So he would pour out his fury on the people completely (compare [Ezekiel 11:21](https://ref.ly/Ezek11:21)).

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ezekiel 23:1–49 (id: 11598)

This chapter, like [chapter 16](https://ref.ly/Ezek16:1-Ezek16:63), tells the history of the northern and southern kingdoms of Israel and Judah. It does this through an extended metaphor (a comparison that continues through many verses).

In this comparison, Samaria and Jerusalem, the capital cities, are described as two immoral women. The language is strong and direct.

This metaphor shows that their judgment was certain and deserved.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 23:1-49

## Ezekiel 23:4 (id: 11608)

Marriage is commonly used in the Bible as a symbol for the covenant relationship between God and his people (for example, [Isaiah 54:1–8](https://ref.ly/Isa54:1-Isa54:8); [Ephesians 5:22–33](https://ref.ly/Eph5:22-Eph5:33)). Adultery symbolizes Israel’s spiritual unfaithfulness (for example, [Hosea 1–3](https://ref.ly/Hos1:1-Hos3:5)). God makes his covenants even though his people are sinful. He does not choose them because of their good character ([Romans 5:6–11](https://ref.ly/Rom5:6-Rom5:11)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 23:4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Oholah (ID: `person:Oholah`); Oholibah (ID: `person:Oholibah`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Samaria (ID: `place:Samaria`)

## Ezekiel 23:22–24 (id: 11601)

Jerusalem’s sin was very great. Because of this, God’s judgment could not be avoided.

The same nations that Jerusalem had trusted and depended on would now attack her. Babylon would come with its allies, Pekod, Shoa, and Koa.

These tribal names are not well\-known. However, they sound like Hebrew words that mean “punishment,” “war cry,” and “shriek” (a loud cry of fear or pain).

Judah’s sins were worse than those of her sister kingdom. So her punishment would also be worse.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 23:22-24

## Ezekiel 23:25–29 (id: 11602)

In the ancient world, people sometimes punished an adulterous wife by stripping her naked. This public act exposed in public what she had done in private (her unfaithfulness in marriage). This was meant to bring shame as punishment for adultery (see study note on Ezekiel 16:35–38). 

In a similar way, the Babylonians took away everything valuable from Jerusalem and Judah. They left the people exposed and ashamed before other nations.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 23:25-29
* **Associated Articles:** Ezekiel 16:35-38 (ID: `11518@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Fornication (ID: `keyterm:Fornication`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`)

## Ezekiel 23:31–34 (id: 11604)

*You will drink your sister’s cup*: Jerusalem would have to drink from this bitter cup of judgment, just like Samaria did. The pain from this judgment would make her beat (or *tear*) her breast in anguish.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 23:31-34

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Samaria (ID: `place:Samaria`)

## Ezekiel 23:36–43 (id: 11605)

The prophet again took the role of a prosecuting attorney. A prosecuting attorney is a person who brings charges against someone in court. His task was to confront Jerusalem with her sins (detailed in [Ezekiel 22](https://ref.ly/Ezek22:1-Ezek22:31)). 

Jerusalem and Samaria were supposed to be holy cities, set apart for God. Instead, they had become like worn\-out prostitutes. This picture shows that they had been unfaithful to God. Their only attraction was their availability, meaning they were ready to give themselves to anyone.

The prophet used this strong image to show how serious their unfaithfulness was. They had turned away from the Lord and given their loyalty to other gods and foreign nations.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 23:36-43

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`); Sabeans (ID: `group:Sabean`); Oholibah (ID: `person:Oholibah`); Play the Prostitute (ID: `keyterm:PlayTheProstitute`); Sheba (ID: `person:Sheba`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Oholah (ID: `person:Oholah`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Fornication (ID: `keyterm:Fornication`); Crown (ID: `realia:Crown`); Olive Oil (ID: `realia:OliveOil`); Bed (ID: `realia:Bed`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Anointing Oil (ID: `realia:AnointingOil`)

## Ezekiel 24:1–14 (id: 11611)

At first, the picture of a cooking pot seems positive. It makes people think about good food and shared meals. In many cultures, a meal brings people together in peace and joy.

The pot was filled with choice pieces of meat from a sacrifice. A fire burned under the pot. The meat began to cook slowly.

However, like many parables, this story has a sharp ending. What should have been a good meal became rotten and unclean. The best pieces of meat went into the pot, but they came out spoiled.

The pot represents Jerusalem ([Ezekiel 24:9](https://ref.ly/Ezek24:9)). The meat inside represents the people. Instead of being pure and pleasing to God, they had become corrupt (morally ruined). As a result, the pot and its contents would be burned and destroyed.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 24:1-14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe`); Complete (ID: `keyterm:Complete`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Cooking Pot (ID: `realia:CookingPot`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); Proverb (ID: `keyterm:Saying`); Pure (ID: `keyterm:Pure`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Lot (ID: `keyterm:Lot`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Ezekiel 24:2 (id: 11616)

* Ezekiel had warned the people about this event, and it had finally happened (see also [2 Kings 25:1–2](https://ref.ly/2Kgs25:1-2Kgs25:2)).
* *has laid siege* (literally *is* *leaning on*): The same words were used when a worshiper placed his hands on the animal he brought for sacrifice ([Leviticus 1:4](https://ref.ly/Lev1:4); [3:2](https://ref.ly/Lev3:2)).

    When the worshiper pressed his hands on the animal, he showed that the animal was taking his place.

    In this picture, Jerusalem is like that animal. The city is shown as a sacrificial lamb that will be killed. Its destruction would display God’s glory and justice.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 24:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`)

## Ezekiel 24:3 (id: 11617)

The people of Jerusalem had rebelled against their treaty with Babylon. A treaty is a formal agreement between two nations.

They had also broken their covenant with the Lord. A covenant is a solemn promise or binding agreement between God and his people.

By breaking both agreements, they showed that they were unfaithful to human rulers and to God.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 24:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Proverb (ID: `keyterm:Saying`); Cooking Pot (ID: `realia:CookingPot`)

## Ezekiel 24:6–8 (id: 11618)

The cooking pot was too dirty to clean. Jerusalem was full of blood that she had shed and left uncovered. The Old Testament required that people cover the blood of animals killed for meat with earth ([Leviticus 17:13](https://ref.ly/Lev17:13)). By leaving the blood of innocent victims uncovered, Jerusalem was doubly guilty. Her own blood would rightly be splashed on the rocks (compare [Psalm 137:8–9](https://ref.ly/Ps137:8-Ps137:9)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 24:6-8

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe`); Lot (ID: `keyterm:Lot`); Cooking Pot (ID: `realia:CookingPot`)

## Ezekiel 24:9–12 (id: 11619)

God said he would make Jerusalem like a red\-hot cooking pot, as if it were in a refiner's furnace. However, even this fire would not remove its corruption, because Jerusalem's impurity was permanent.

## Ezekiel 24:15–17 (id: 11613)

Nowhere do we see a prophet so deeply involved in his message as in this event. God took the life of Ezekiel’s wife. Yet Ezekiel was not allowed to mourn her openly, even though she was very precious to him.

Ezekiel was a priest ([Ezekiel 1:3](https://ref.ly/Ezek1:3)). Priests had strict rules about mourning. When people mourned, they often tore their clothes and left their hair uncombed. These public signs of grief made a person ceremonially unclean (not fit for worship).

Priests were not allowed to make themselves unclean, except for their closest family members ([Leviticus 21:1–4](https://ref.ly/Lev21:1-Lev21:4)). Even so, God told Ezekiel not to follow the usual customs of mourning.

Ezekiel’s silence was a sign act (a symbolic action that carries a message). It showed what would soon happen to Israel ([Ezekiel 24:20–24](https://ref.ly/Ezek24:20-Ezek24:24); see “Prophetic Sign Acts” Theme Note). The people would lose something precious, but they would not be able to mourn in the normal way.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 24:15-17
* **Associated Articles:** Prophetic Sign Acts (ID: `500313@TyndaleStudyNotesThemes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Plague (ID: `keyterm:Blow`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`)

## Ezekiel 24:20–24 (id: 11614)

Ezekiel's lack of mourning ([Ezekiel 24:15–17](https://ref.ly/Ezek24:15-Ezek24:17)) was a sign to Israel about their future. The Lord was about to take away the temple of Jerusalem, the place they cherished. God would desecrate it and destroy the sons and daughters left in Jerusalem. 

On that day, the people would act like Ezekiel. They would not mourn publicly or perform rituals. Although they would feel deep loss, the devastation would be so great that normal mourning rites would be impossible. In such complete desolation, only internal grief would be possible.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 24:20-24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Ezekiel (ID: `person:Ezekiel`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Symbol (ID: `keyterm:Example`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`)

## Ezekiel 24:25–27 (id: 11615)

During this deep gloom and sorrow, when a survivor confirmed the fall of Jerusalem, there would be a sign of hope. On that day, Ezekiel's voice would return (see [Ezekiel 3:26](https://ref.ly/Ezek3:26)), allowing him to pray to God for the people and intercede for them. The final destruction of Jerusalem would mark the complete outpouring of God's wrath and fury. On that day, Ezekiel would speak words of hope to the shattered exiles, helping them know the Lord.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 24:25-27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Symbol (ID: `keyterm:Example`); Fortress (ID: `realia:Fortress`)

## Ezekiel 25:1–32.32 (id: 11620)

* This section contains a series of messages of judgment against nearby nations. There are six shorter messages against Judah's immediate neighbors, arranged in a clockwise order, followed by a final message against Egypt ([Ezekiel 30–32](https://ref.ly/Ezek30:1-Ezek32:32)). Through experiencing God's judgment, the nations would recognize God's control over everything. The nations might be used to carry out God's judgment on his people, but they would not avoid judgment themselves.
* One of the main purposes of these messages was to show that the negative part of God's promise to Abraham ("I will … curse those who curse you," [Genesis 12:3](https://ref.ly/Gen12:3)) was still active. No one can harm God's people and avoid consequences, even when God's people are under his judgment.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 25:1-32:32

## Ezekiel 25:3–7 (id: 11623)

The Ammonites celebrated Judah's downfall and the destruction of Israel's temple. So, as the prophet warned ([Ezekiel 21:28–32](https://ref.ly/Ezek21:28-Ezek21:32)), they would face invasion and destruction. Others would consume the Ammonites' produce, and their people would be wiped out, similar to Judah. The Ammonites' gods could not save them from the Lord's anger, and they would realize that the Lord is the true God.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 25:3-7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Ammon (ID: `place:Ammon`); East (ID: `place:East`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Ammonites (ID: `group:Ammon`); To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Camel (ID: `fauna:Camel`)

## Ezekiel 25:8–11 (id: 11624)

The people of Moab believed they could attack Judah without punishment. They treated Judah as if it were just another nation.

This was not true. Judah had been acting like the other nations (see [Ezekiel 20:32](https://ref.ly/Ezek20:32)). However, Judah was different because it had a covenant with God. 

God would not finally reject Judah. He would discipline his people, but he would not abandon them forever.

Moab, however, would face judgment. Like Ammon, Moab would be removed from the register of the nations (the official list of recognized nations). It would be left desolate (empty and ruined) forever.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 25:8-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Moab (ID: `place:Moab`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Kiriathaim (ID: `place:Kiriathaim`); East (ID: `place:East`); Seir (ID: `place:Seir`); Ammon (ID: `place:Ammon`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Baal-meon (ID: `place:Baal-meon`); Ammonites (ID: `group:Ammon`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Beth-jeshimoth (ID: `place:Beth-jeshimoth`)

## Ezekiel 25:12–14 (id: 11621)

* Ammon and Moab were happy about Judah's downfall, but Edom actively took part in it (see [Ezekiel 35:5](https://ref.ly/Ezek35:5); [Obadiah 1](https://ref.ly/Obad1:1-Obad1:21)).
* *vengefully*: The people of Edom captured those fleeing and handed over survivors to help the Babylonians ([Obadiah 1:11–14](https://ref.ly/Obad1:11-Obad1:14)). They were opportunists, settling old conflicts from the ancient feud between Jacob and Esau ([Genesis 27:41](https://ref.ly/Gen27:41)). They took what they could from Judah’s suffering. In return, the Lord would destroy their land.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 25:12-14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Edom (ID: `place:Edom`); Dedan (ID: `place:Dedan`); Teman (ID: `place:Teman`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`)

## Ezekiel 25:15–17 (id: 11622)

* The Philistines had a long\-standing dislike for Judah. Their specific wrongdoings are not mentioned, but the Lord noticed them and would seek revenge. They would then understand the Lord's supreme power.
* The Kerethites were a Philistine tribe (see [1 Samuel 30:14](https://ref.ly/1Sam30:14)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 25:15-17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`); Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`); Cherethites (ID: `group:Cherethite`)

## Ezekiel 26:1 (id: 11630)

February 3, 585 BC was about seven months after the fall of Jerusalem.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 26:1

## Ezekiel 26:1–28.19 (id: 11625)

The message against Tyre, Israel’s northwestern neighbor, is more detailed than the short messages before it. It consists of three similar sections ([Ezekiel 26:2–21](https://ref.ly/Ezek26:2-Ezek26:21); [27:1–36](https://ref.ly/Ezek27:1-Ezek27:36); [and 28:1–19](https://ref.ly/Ezek28:1-Ezek28:19)). Each section conveys the same idea: Tyre would meet a terrible end and cease to exist ([Ezekiel 27:36](https://ref.ly/Ezek27:36)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 26:1-28:19

## Ezekiel 26:2 (id: 11631)

Like its neighbors, Tyre celebrated when Jerusalem fell. This event removed a competing trade center and possibly opened new trade routes and markets for Tyre.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 26:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Tyre (ID: `place:Tyre`)

## Ezekiel 26:3–6 (id: 11632)

* The nations Tyre wanted to trade with would instead attack her, prepared for war. Like Jerusalem, she would become loot for their armies.
* *as the sea brings up its waves*: This image fits Tyre very well. Tyre was built on a small island along the coast. Waves were a normal part of life there.

    The picture shows how enemies would strike Tyre again and again, like waves hitting the shore. It is a metaphor (a word picture) for a military assault.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 26:3-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Tyre (ID: `place:Tyre`); Tower (ID: `realia:Tower`); Net (ID: `realia:Net`); Dragnet (ID: `realia:Dragnet`)

## Ezekiel 26:7–11 (id: 11633)

The detailed description of Tyre's expected destruction shows confidence in the conflict's outcome.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 26:7-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`); Chariot (ID: `realia:Chariot`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); Tyre (ID: `place:Tyre`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Nebuchadnezzar (ID: `person:Nebuchadnezzar`); Battering-Ram (ID: `realia:Battering-ram`); Tower (ID: `realia:Tower`); Wagon (ID: `realia:Wagon`); Large Shield (ID: `realia:LargeShield`); Siege-Works (ID: `realia:Siege-works`); Sacred Pillar (ID: `realia:SacredPillar`); Siege Ramp (ID: `realia:SiegeRamp`)

## Ezekiel 26:12–14 (id: 11626)

In the end, events happened just as the prophet had described earlier in picture language (metaphor). Tyre would become a bare rock. It would no longer be a great and wealthy city.

Instead of being a busy center for ships and caravans from the east, it would become a quiet place. Local fishermen would spread their nets there to dry ([Ezekiel 26:2](https://ref.ly/Ezek26:2)). The great trading city would become empty and ruined.

The Jewish historian Josephus wrote that Nebuchadnezzar besieged Tyre for 13 years. However, Tyre was not completely destroyed at that time.

It was finally destroyed later by Alexander the Great in 332 BC.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 26:12-14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Net (ID: `realia:Net`); Lute (ID: `realia:Lute`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Dragnet (ID: `realia:Dragnet`)

## Ezekiel 26:15–16 (id: 11627)

The fall of Tyre would not affect Tyre alone. It would also harm the cities and nations that traded with her.

Tyre was an important center for trade along the coast. Many rulers depended on her for wealth and goods. When Tyre fell, the economic impact (financial damage) would spread along the whole coastline.

Because of this loss, some rulers would abdicate (give up their rule and step down from power).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 26:15-16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Tyre (ID: `place:Tyre`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Throne (ID: `realia:Throne`); Robe (ID: `realia:Robe`); Embroidered Cloth (ID: `realia:EmbroideredCloth`)

## Ezekiel 26:17–18 (id: 11628)

* A funeral song would be sung for Tyre. This kind of song is called a lament (a song of mourning for the dead). The prophet had used this kind of song earlier (see study note on Ezekiel 19:1–14). People would repeat this funeral song from place to place. Many would mourn the fall of the great city.
* *powerful on the sea … who imposed terror*: Tyre had strong naval power (military strength at sea). Her ships traveled widely and controlled important trade routes.

    Because of this power, Tyre spread fear among other nations. Her trading system was not always peaceful. It was often based on conquest (defeating others in war), subjugation (forcing others to submit), and exploitation (using others unfairly for gain). See [Ezekiel 28:16](https://ref.ly/Ezek28:16), [18](https://ref.ly/Ezek28:18).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 26:17-18
* **Associated Articles:** Ezekiel 19:1-14 (ID: `11548@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lamentation (ID: `keyterm:Lamentation`)

## Ezekiel 26:19–21 (id: 11629)

God would show his sovereign power by completely destroying Tyre.

It would be as if the great city had sunk beneath the deep waters of the sea. This picture describes total ruin. The people of Tyre would go down to the pit (the place of the dead), where the wicked dead remain. They would never return.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 26:19-21

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ezekiel 27:1–36 (id: 11634)

The second part of the prophet’s message to Tyre (see study note on Ezekiel 26:1–28:19) is a funeral song. 

The song compares Tyre’s past glory with its present loss. It connects with the previous chapter in several ways. Both speak directly to Tyre. Both describe Tyre as a gateway and a center of trade (see [Ezekiel 26:1–2](https://ref.ly/Ezek26:1-Ezek26:2)). Both end with the same message: Tyre has come to a terrible end and will exist no more (compare [Ezekiel 26:21](https://ref.ly/Ezek26:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 27:1-36
* **Associated Articles:** Ezekiel 26:1-28.19 (ID: `11625@TyndaleStudyNotes`)
