## Ezekiel 27:12–25 (id: 11637)

This large trading system existed to serve Tyre’s strong desire for trade. The city wanted goods from many different lands and did not seem satisfied. The list of Tyre’s trading partners is very long. Tyre traded in many kinds of goods, including slaves, horses, saddle blankets, silver, dyed cloth, and figs (compare [Revelation 18:11–13](https://ref.ly/Rev18:11-Rev18:13)). 

The cargo list is arranged by geographic region. It names places from every direction (north, south, east, and west). It includes both large and small trading centers. Almost every valuable object that people could buy or sell appears somewhere on the list of Tyre’s goods.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 27:12-25

Sheba was a kingdom in southwest Arabia. The location of Raamah is uncertain, but it was always associated with Sheba.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 27:22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Raamah (ID: `place:Raamah`); Sheba (ID: `place:Sheba.2`); Balsam Tree (ID: `flora:BalsamTree`)

## Ezekiel 27:23 (id: 11646)

Haran, Canneh, Eden, and Asshur were all located in Mesopotamia. Mesopotamia is in what is now modern Iraq.

Chilmad is not mentioned anywhere else in ancient writings. Because of this, some scholars think the name may be a scribal error. A scribal error is a copying mistake made by a writer. It may have originally meant “all Media,” a region northeast of Mesopotamia.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 27:23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Haran (ID: `place:Haran`); Eden (ID: `place:Eden`); Calneh (ID: `place:Calneh`); Chilmad (ID: `place:Chilmad`); Assyria (ID: `place:Assyria`); Sheba (ID: `place:Sheba.2`)

## Ezekiel 27:26 (id: 11647)

The rich merchant ship seemed impossible to sink. It was loaded with valuable goods and appeared strong and secure.

But it could not stand against the strong east wind. This “east wind” represents the army of Babylon. The army would destroy Tyre, just as a powerful storm destroys a ship.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 27:26


## Ezekiel 27:36 (id: 11648)

Tyre's former residents and trading partners mourn the loss of its way of life. This section, like the previous one, ends by stating that Tyre has met a terrible end and will no longer exist (compare [Ezekiel 26:21](https://ref.ly/Ezek26:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 27:36


## Ezekiel 28:1–19 (id: 11653)

* This section is the third message against Tyre (see study note on Ezekiel 26:1–28:19). It speaks directly to the ruler of Tyre, called the prince of Tyre. It condemns him for his pride.

    The prince represents the city itself. What happens to him also happens to Tyre. In his pride, he claimed to be a god. He said that he sat on a divine throne and ruled over the powerful seas. But this was not true. He was only a man.

* This chapter and [Isaiah 14](https://ref.ly/Isa14:1-Isa14:32), which speaks about the king of Babylon, are sometimes understood as describing a heavenly battle between God and Satan, who is called “the prince of demons” ([Matthew 12:24](https://ref.ly/Matt12:24)). However, this interpretation overlooks the historical setting of these passages.

    Tyre and Babylon were real cities. Their kings were real men. They had great power, but they were also very proud. The king of Tyre claimed to be a god, but his claim was empty.

    Political powers that oppose God and his people may serve as agents of Satan in his struggle against God. Yet these rulers will fall. Their defeat points forward to God’s final victory over all the powers of evil. Every authority that sets itself against the living God will be destroyed.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 28:1-19
* **Associated Articles:** Ezekiel 26:1-28.19 (ID: `11625@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Tyre (ID: `place:Tyre`); To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`); Winged Creature (ID: `realia:WingedCreature`); Understanding (ID: `keyterm:Understanding`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`); Garden of Eden (ID: `place:Eden.2`); Hell (ID: `place:Hell`); Lamentation (ID: `keyterm:Lamentation`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Uncircumcised Person (ID: `keyterm:UncircumcisedPerson`); Mighty (ID: `keyterm:Mighty.2`); Complete (ID: `keyterm:Complete`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Drum (ID: `realia:Drum`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`)

## Ezekiel 28:3–5 (id: 11662)

The prince of Tyre claimed to be divine because of his wisdom and his wealth. He believed that his wisdom had made him very rich.

His riches then made him extremely proud. His pride grew out of his success and wealth.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 28:3-5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Understanding (ID: `keyterm:Understanding`); Daniel (ID: `person:Daniel.3`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Ezekiel 28:6–7 (id: 11663)

The prince of Tyre's pride led to his downfall ([Proverbs 16:18](https://ref.ly/Prov16:18)). His claims of wisdom and power would prove empty when the Lord sent the Babylonian army against him. They would draw their swords and humble him.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 28:6-7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Mighty (ID: `keyterm:Mighty.2`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`)

## Ezekiel 28:8 (id: 11664)

The clearest proof that the prince of Tyre was only a mortal man came when the Babylonians killed him. He would not rest in the heights with the gods, as he had claimed. Instead, he would go down to the pit, the place of the dead.

Like the city of Tyre, the prince would die in "the heart of the sea" (compare [Ezekiel 27:26–27](https://ref.ly/Ezek27:26-Ezek27:27)). This image means he would share the same complete destruction as the city.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 28:8

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Hell (ID: `place:Hell`)

## Ezekiel 28:10 (id: 11654)

*will die the death of the uncircumcised*: In the Old Testament, circumcision was the sign of God’s covenant (his special agreement) with Abraham and his descendants (compare [Genesis 17:10–14](https://ref.ly/Gen17:10-Gen17:14)). To be *uncircumcised* meant to stand outside that covenant relationship.

This warning means the prince of Tyre would die without any share in God’s covenant promises. He would die as one who did not belong to the people of the covenant.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 28:10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Uncircumcised Person (ID: `keyterm:UncircumcisedPerson`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ezekiel 28:12–19 (id: 11655)

At first, this funeral song seems to treat the prince of Tyre’s claim to be divine as if it were true. It describes him as the model of perfection, full of wisdom and beauty.

But the tone is not sincere. The lament is sarcastic. It uses praise to expose his pride and to show how far he had fallen.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 28:12-19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Winged Creature (ID: `realia:WingedCreature`); Tyre (ID: `place:Tyre`); Complete (ID: `keyterm:Complete`); Lamentation (ID: `keyterm:Lamentation`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Garden of Eden (ID: `place:Eden.2`); Drum (ID: `realia:Drum`)

## Ezekiel 28:13–14 (id: 11656)

* Ezekiel mocks Tyre’s claim to great age and importance. He describes the king as if he had been in Eden at the beginning of the world. He presents him as a powerful angelic guardian. These heavenly beings carried the Lord’s throne in [Ezekiel 1](https://ref.ly/Ezek1:1-Ezek1:28) and guarding the garden in [Genesis 3](https://ref.ly/Gen3:1-Gen3:24).

    In this picture, the king had access to the holy mountain of God. In the Bible, mountains are often linked with God’s presence.

* The *fiery stones* may refer to shining gemstones around the garden of Eden. The list of jewels that the prince of Tyre supposedly wore adds to this image. It suggests he was specially chosen. The list includes nine of the twelve stones on the high priest’s breastplate in [Exodus 28](https://ref.ly/Exod28:1-Exod28:43).

    This description mocks the prince’s claim. It suggests that he believed he had a place even higher than Adam’s. He claimed a place among the divine beings themselves.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 28:13-14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Garden of Eden (ID: `place:Eden.2`); Drum (ID: `realia:Drum`); Winged Creature (ID: `realia:WingedCreature`)

## Ezekiel 28:15–18 (id: 11657)

This sarcastic description of the prince of Tyre's greatness and pride sets him up for his fall, similar to the fall of humanity ([Genesis 3](https://ref.ly/Gen3:1-Gen3:24)). Like Adam, the king of Tyre's supposedly blameless state was temporary and ended abruptly when evil was found in him. His wealth and dishonest trade led him to violence (compare [Ezekiel 26:17](https://ref.ly/Ezek26:17)). 

Someone who claimed to be greater than Adam could experience a similar fall from favor and be banished from the mountain of God, the place of God's favor. The prince of Tyre's God\-given beauty and wisdom were corrupted by his pride, leading to disaster and revealing his true nature.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 28:15-18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`); Complete (ID: `keyterm:Complete`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Winged Creature (ID: `realia:WingedCreature`)

## Ezekiel 28:18–19 (id: 11658)

The prince of Tyre was not a god who could make land holy. Instead, he made his sanctuaries unclean. Earlier, judgment was declared on his city. The prince of Tyre was destined to meet a terrible end and disappear (compare [Ezekiel 26:21](https://ref.ly/Ezek26:21); [27:36](https://ref.ly/Ezek27:36)). The proud leader would fall with his grand ship, reduced to nothing by the Lord's action.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 28:18-19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`)

## Ezekiel 28:20–24 (id: 11659)

No specific charges are made against Sidon, Tyre’s close neighbor to the north. However, it likely committed similar offenses. Its joy over Judah’s destruction would not last.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 28:20-24

## Ezekiel 28:22–23 (id: 11660)

The Lord planned to show his glory and holiness through judgment. He would bring three kinds of punishment on Sidon: 

* plague (disease),
* bloodshed, and
* attack by the sword.

The attack would come from every side. No part of the city would be safe.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 28:22-23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Sidon (ID: `place:Sidon`); Bring Honor and Glory on Oneself (ID: `keyterm:BringHonorAndGloryOnOneself`)

## Ezekiel 28:25–26 (id: 11661)

The Lord would not show his holiness only by judging nations for their pride, arrogance, and hostility toward his chosen people. He would also bring his people back to Israel. There, they would live safely, build homes, and plant vineyards. These were common signs of covenant blessings in the Old Testament (see [Micah 4:4](https://ref.ly/Mic4:4); [Zechariah 3:10](https://ref.ly/Zech3:10)). 

After God punished the neighboring nations and restored his people, they would find peace. The nations would recognize God as the Sovereign Lord through his powerful judgments, and Israel would know him as the Lord their God. This title reflects God's covenant relationship of worship and fellowship with them.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 28:25-26

## Ezekiel 29:1 (id: 11669)

This day in the tenth year since the exile of Jehoiachin was approximately one year after the beginning of the siege of Jerusalem.

## Ezekiel 29:1–32.32 (id: 11665)

* The seventh and final message of judgment against the nations targets Egypt, an old enemy and ally of Israel. This message is the longest and, like the one against Tyre, it addresses both Egypt and its ruler.
* Egypt often tempted Israel and Judah to turn away from their loyalty to the Lord. Soon after leaving Egypt, Israel fell into the trap of Egypt's idolatry. Egypt led Judah to rely on chariots and horses instead of the Lord, but Egypt was unreliable when it mattered most. The Lord judged Egypt for leading his people astray (compare [Luke 17:1–2](https://ref.ly/Luke17:1-Luke17:2)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 29:1-32:32

## Ezekiel 29:3–16 (id: 11671)

The message of judgment begins with a judgment against Pharaoh, who is called a great sea monster. In the ancient Near East, sea monsters were common in stories about the gods. These creatures represented chaos, which the gods had to control. In [Genesis 1:21](https://ref.ly/Gen1:21), however, sea creatures appear as part of God’s good creation. They are not enemies of God.

In this chapter, the image changes slightly. The sea monster also reflects Pharaoh as a great crocodile. He rests confidently in the streams that flow through the Nile River delta.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 29:3-16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Nile River (ID: `place:Nile`); Fish (ID: `fauna:Fish`); Syene (ID: `place:Syene`); Pathros (ID: `place:Pathros`); Safety (ID: `keyterm:Safety`); Hophra (ID: `person:Hophra`); Cush (ID: `place:Cush.2`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Migdol (ID: `place:Migdol`); Dragon (ID: `fauna:Dragon`); Harpoon (ID: `realia:Harpoon`); Reed (ID: `flora:Reed`); Staff (ID: `realia:Staff`)

## Ezekiel 29:4–5 (id: 11672)

The image of the powerful crocodile leads to another image. The Lord will come like a great hunter.

Like Tyre, Egypt trusted in its watery defenses. But those waters would not protect it. The king of Egypt, who seemed strong and frightening, would be captured like an ordinary crocodile.

He would be dragged into the wilderness, which is a place of judgment. His allies are pictured as fish taken with him. There he would die. His body would be left in the open for wild animals and birds to eat.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 29:4-5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fish (ID: `fauna:Fish`); Harpoon (ID: `realia:Harpoon`)

## Ezekiel 29:6–7 (id: 11673)

Egypt’s sin is closely connected to Israel. Egypt is described as a staff made of reeds. A reed staff looks strong but breaks easily.

Egypt claimed it would support Israel, but it did not have the power or the will to help. It encouraged Israel to rebel against Assyria and Babylon but did not provide real help (compare [2 Kings 18:21](https://ref.ly/2Kgs18:21)). 

Israel sinned by trusting in this weak support. But Egypt was also guilty. It gave false hope and would face God’s judgment.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 29:6-7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Staff (ID: `realia:Staff`); Reed (ID: `flora:Reed`)

## Ezekiel 29:8–13 (id: 11674)

God announced that he would destroy the land of Egypt. The land would become empty and ruined. The destruction would stretch from Migdol in the north to Aswan in the south. This means the whole land of Egypt.

Egypt would be empty for 40 years (compare [Ezekiel 4:6](https://ref.ly/Ezek4:6)). God would scatter the Egyptians among other nations. Later, he would bring them back to their land. 

The Babylonians likely invaded Egypt in 568 or 567 BC. They took prisoners, who remained in exile until the time of Cyrus. This was about one generation later.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 29:8-13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Syene (ID: `place:Syene`); Nile River (ID: `place:Nile`); Cush (ID: `place:Cush.2`); Migdol (ID: `place:Migdol`)

## Ezekiel 29:14–16 (id: 11666)

Judah would one day be fully restored. Egypt, however, would return as a weak and unimportant kingdom.

Israel would no longer trust Egypt for help. Instead, they would learn to trust the Lord. Egypt’s reduced position would remind Israel of its past mistake. They had trusted Egypt instead of trusting God.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 29:14-16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pathros (ID: `place:Pathros`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Safety (ID: `keyterm:Safety`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

This message came in the twenty\-seventh year of King Jehoiachin’s captivity. This is the latest dated message in the book of Ezekiel. It is even later than Ezekiel’s vision of the temple in[Ezekiel 40–48](https://ref.ly/Ezek40:1-Ezek48:35).

## Ezekiel 29:18–20 (id: 11668)

Nebuchadnezzar attacked Tyre ([Ezekiel 26–28](https://ref.ly/Ezek26:1-Ezek28:26)). His army worked very hard but gained little wealth from the city.

The Lord said that workers deserve their wages. So he would give Egypt to Babylon as payment for their work. Egypt would become their reward.

## Ezekiel 29:21 (id: 11670)

* Even when God judged other nations, he still focused on his own people. As Egypt grew weaker, God would make Israel strong again.
* *I will cause a horn to sprout for the house of Israel**:* A horn was a common symbol of strength and dignity (see [Psalm 132:17](https://ref.ly/Ps132:17); compare [Daniel 7:7–8](https://ref.ly/Dan7:7-Dan7:8); [8:3](https://ref.ly/Dan8:3)). This means that God would restore Israel’s strength and dignity.

    This restoration would also show that Ezekiel was a true prophet. Ezekiel had served the Lord faithfully. At times, people may have doubted him. In the end, they would see that his message was true.

## Ezekiel 30:21–23 (id: 11698)

*broken the arm of Pharaoh*: The Lord had already weakened the Egyptians' power when Nebuchadnezzar defeated Pharaoh Hophra (see [Jeremiah 37:5–11](https://ref.ly/Jer37:5-Jer37:11)). If Hophra had succeeded, Jerusalem would have had temporary relief. 

Now, all hope for help from Egypt was gone. There was no chance for the broken arm to heal or even be temporarily supported, so Pharaoh could not protect Jerusalem. Egypt would be completely helpless, unable to wield a sword as it awaited its final defeat.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 30:21-23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Hophra (ID: `person:Hophra`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ezekiel 30:24–26 (id: 11699)

While weakening Pharaoh ([Ezekiel 30:22](https://ref.ly/Ezek30:22)), the Lord would strengthen the arms of Babylon's king, making the contest even more uneven. The Lord controlled the fate of the upcoming battle between the superpowers and had already decided its outcome. Nebuchadnezzar fought the Egyptians several times, ending with victory in 567 BC.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 30:24-26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Hophra (ID: `person:Hophra`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`)

## Ezekiel 31:1–18 (id: 11701)

Ezekiel asked the Egyptians to compare themselves to Assyria, which was like a great tree in Eden ([Ezekiel 31:9](https://ref.ly/Ezek31:9)). If that tree was cut down and sent to the underworld, how could Egypt, whose glory was less than Assyria's, believe it could survive?

## Ezekiel 31:6 (id: 11708)

Like a great tree, Assyria provided shelter for all of the birds and wild animals of the earth. This tree was more magnificent than all the trees in the garden of God, the garden of Eden. It had a God\-given beauty and stature similar to the prince of Tyre in [Ezekiel 28](https://ref.ly/Ezek28:1-Ezek28:26). Assyria's power was so great that all the great nations of the world lived under its influence.

## Ezekiel 32:2–3 (id: 11715)

* Ezekiel compares Pharaoh to a powerful beast ([Ezekiel 29:3](https://ref.ly/Ezek29:3)).
* Egypt's pharaohs used the *lion* and *a monster in the seas* (or *crocodile*) as symbols of strength. These animals represented power and control. However, both lions and crocodiles can be hunted and killed. The same would happen to Pharaoh.

    God would act through his chosen agents ([Ezekiel 32:11–12](https://ref.ly/Ezek32:11-Ezek32:12)). They would hunt Pharaoh, capture him, and drag him away.

## Ezekiel 32:4–6 (id: 11718)

*mountains … valleys … mountains … ravines*: In Hebrew, this is a literary device called a *merism* (a way of naming the outer parts to include everything in between). It shows both the boundaries and all that lies within them. Here, it emphasizes that God’s judgment would cover the whole land.

The description of destruction uses hyperbole (intentional exaggeration). This strong language shows that Egypt’s defeat would be complete.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 32:4-6


## Ezekiel 32:7–8 (id: 11719)

As in the previous chapter, Pharaoh’s downfall would bring global darkness and widespread mourning. These images were often linked to the day of the Lord (see [Joel 2:30](https://ref.ly/Joel2:30)). In this case, the darkness would also remind the Egyptians of the plague during the exodus ([Exodus 10:21–22](https://ref.ly/Exod10:21-Exod10:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 32:7-8

## Ezekiel 33:2–4 (id: 11725)

*watchman*: See study note on Ezekiel 3:16219. The message about the watchman is part of Ezekiel’s public announcement, not a private task. It emphasizes that the listeners are responsible for acting on the watchman’s message. 

Before Jerusalem's destruction, people ignored the warning. After the exile, they struggled to believe the message of hope. In both situations, Ezekiel faced rejection and was tempted to stay silent. God warned him again that he could not.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 33:2-4

## Ezekiel 33:5–9 (id: 11732)

Ezekiel's message urged the people of Israel to repent so they could live. The Lord had warned of an army coming against their country, and this was happening. Ezekiel had been faithful as a watchman. No one who heard his prophecy could say he did not warn them of the coming judgment. But the people did not listen to the warning. If they did not turn away from their sin and return to God, they would die in their sins.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 33:5-9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Rams Horn (ID: `realia:RamsHorn`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Ezekiel 33:10–11 (id: 11722)

*our sins are heavy upon us … How can we live?*: At last, the people of Israel began to take the prophet’s warnings seriously. However, there was now a new danger. Instead of turning to God in repentance and faith, they might fall into despair.

God’s judgment is not like an unchangeable fate. It still allows room for forgiveness. The Sovereign Lord does not take pleasure in the death of wicked people.

Even those who have done great evil can live. If they repent (turn away from their sin) and change their ways, they will live.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 33:10-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`)

## Ezekiel 33:12–16 (id: 11723)

The principle in [Ezekiel 33:10–11](https://ref.ly/Ezek33:10-Ezek33:11) is explained through two examples.

The first example concerns righteous people. Some trust in their past good behavior to save them. But later they turn to sin. God says he will judge them for their present sin, even if they once lived righteously. Their past obedience will not protect them if they now reject what is right.

The second example concerns wicked people who repent. If they turn away from their evil actions and begin to do what is just and right, God will forgive them completely. No matter what they did in the past, they will live if they truly change.

This teaching repeats an earlier message. In [Ezekiel 18](https://ref.ly/Ezek18:1-Ezek18:32), the people said they were suffering because of their parents’ sins. Here, they seem to say something different. They think their parents’ sins have placed the nation under a permanent curse. Because of this, they believe repentance is useless.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 33:12-16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Trust (ID: `keyterm:LeanOn`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`)

## Ezekiel 33:17–20 (id: 11724)

* *The way of the Lord is not just*: People thought it was wrong for God to use Babylon to destroy Jerusalem. The Lord quickly corrected this idea ([Ezekiel 33:18](https://ref.ly/Ezek33:18)).
* *their way that is not just*: The people's mistake was refusing to repent and accusing God of being unjust.
* *I will judge each of you according to his ways*: The Lord's judgment is never random but matches their sins. If they repent, trust the Lord for salvation, and act justly and rightly, they have hope for the future (compare [Ephesians 2:8–10](https://ref.ly/Eph2:8-Eph2:10)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 33:17-20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); The Way (ID: `keyterm:TheWay`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

Those who stayed in the ruined cities of Judah saw the Exile as a chance for personal gain, not repentance. They claimed to be the only heirs of Abraham's promise and sought to own all the land. ## Ezekiel 33:30–33 (id: 11731)

The situation was not much better among the exiles in Babylon.

Many of them talked about Ezekiel’s messages. They discussed what he said, but they did not change their attitudes or behavior. They were no different from the people still living in Judah.

They would sit in front of Ezekiel and appear sincere. However, they did not plan to obey what the Lord told them. They treated his messages as something pleasant to hear.

But the Lord warned them that, in time, the truth would be clear. Events would show that the words of a true prophet carry God’s power.

## Ezekiel 34:1–24 (id: 11733)

This chapter includes both judgment and salvation. God would judge the *shepherds*, that is, the former kings of Judah. They failed to care for their flock, meaning the people of Judah.

The Lord would also judge the *fattened sheep*. These were the strong and selfish people who harmed others. Yet God would act as a good shepherd. He would care for the rest of the flock and provide for them.

The image of the shepherd shows both the strength and kindness of God's actions with His people. In the ancient Near East, a shepherd was a common symbol for a king. People saw the earthly king as a representative of the divine shepherd who appointed him. 

Shepherds had to protect their flocks from wild animals like lions and bears. They also needed to know their sheep by name and gently lead them to good pasture and calm waters. They faced cold, heat, wind, rain, and snow while caring for their flocks. 

Good kings who led their people wisely and strongly were like shepherds. The New Testament uses the same image to describe pastors and elders, who should oversee their flock without being domineering ([1 Peter 5:2–4](https://ref.ly/1Pet5:2-1Pet5:4)). Jesus perfectly combines strength and kindness as the “great Shepherd of the sheep” ([Hebrews 13:20](https://ref.ly/Heb13:20)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 34:1-24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); David (ID: `person:David`); Grass (ID: `flora:Grass`); Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe.2`); Wool (ID: `realia:Wool`)

He would make the land fruitful again. In this way, he would show that his name is honored and holy ([Ezekiel 35–36](https://ref.ly/Ezek35:1-Ezek36:38)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 34:1-31

## Ezekiel 34:2–6 (id: 11737)

*Woe to the shepherds*: Israel’s leaders did not care for the weak or search for those who wandered away and were lost. They pursued their own interests, feeding themselves at the flock’s expense. They ruled the sheep with harshness and cruelty*,* similar to how the Egyptians treated the Israelites in the time of Moses ([Exodus 1:13–14](https://ref.ly/Exod1:13-Exod1:14)). The neglect and abuse by these cruel shepherds scattered the Lord’s flock across the earth.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 34:2-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Woe (ID: `keyterm:Woe.2`); Wool (ID: `realia:Wool`)

## Ezekiel 34:7–11 (id: 11742)

The Lord promised to hold the selfish shepherds accountable for their actions. He would remove them from their positions and save his flock from their grasp, so they would no longer be prey. The Lord would search for his scattered flock ([Ezekiel 34:12](https://ref.ly/Ezek34:12)) and bring them home.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 34:7-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`)

## Ezekiel 34:12–16 (id: 11735)

The dark and cloudy day, the day of judgment (compare [Ezekiel 32:7–8](https://ref.ly/Ezek32:7-Ezek32:8)), was over. Now God would bring his people back to the mountains of Israel, the center of the land promised to the patriarchs, and care for his sheep (see [Psalm 23](https://ref.ly/Ps23:1-Ps23:6)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 34:12-16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Grass (ID: `flora:Grass`)

## Ezekiel 34:17–19 (id: 11736)

The goats were the powerful, unrighteous members of the community.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 34:17-19

## Ezekiel 34:20–22 (id: 11738)

*the fat sheep and the lean sheep*: People with power and influence took all the good things for themselves, leaving others without resources. God will judge them and make things right.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 34:20-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Ezekiel 34:23–24 (id: 11739)

*one shepherd, My servant David*: God had promised to raise up a descendant of David to rule after him ([2 Samuel 7:12–16](https://ref.ly/2Sam7:12-2Sam7:16)). This “new David,” like the first, would serve the Lord. He would be faithful to God and lead the people well. He would care for them like a good shepherd cares for his sheep.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 34:23-24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** David (ID: `person:David`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Ezekiel 34:25–26 (id: 11740)

God promised to give his people a new and better ruler. He would also make a covenant of peace with them.

At that time, they were suffering from wild animals, drought, famine, and war. These troubles were the result of breaking the covenant God made at Mount Sinai (see [Leviticus 26:14–35](https://ref.ly/Lev26:14-Lev26:35)).

From then on, they would live in safety. They would camp without fear. God would send the rain they needed so the land would produce crops. They would enjoy peace and the blessings of the covenant (see [Leviticus 26:4–13](https://ref.ly/Lev26:4-Lev26:13)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 34:25-26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Safety (ID: `keyterm:Safety`); Peace (ID: `keyterm:IntactState`); Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`); Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`); Hyena (ID: `fauna:Hyena`); Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`); Jackal (ID: `fauna:Jackal`); Bear (ID: `fauna:Bear`)

## Ezekiel 34:27–31 (id: 11741)

In this covenant of peace (a promise of restored relationship with God), God’s people receive the blessings that come from being whole in their relationship with him. This covenant was not completely new. It was like the covenant God made at Mount Sinai.

The Sinai covenant promised true and lasting peace. However, the people did not experience that peace because of their sin. They broke their relationship with God again and again.

In the past, Israel’s kings failed to lead the people well. In this new covenant, God would give them a new and perfect king. The broken relationship with God would be restored.

God’s people would again belong to him. They would be like sheep who belong to their shepherd. The covenant would reach its goal when the Sovereign Lord was their God and lived among them once more.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 34:27-31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Safety (ID: `keyterm:Safety`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Yoke (ID: `realia:Yoke`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Ezekiel 35:1–15 (id: 11743)

This message of judgment is directed to Edom, Israel’s neighbor to the southeast. It is also called Mount Seir, the main mountain region of Edom.

Edom stands as a symbol of all Israel’s enemies. For example, Edom rejoiced when Israel fell ([Ezekiel 36:2](https://ref.ly/Ezek36:2); see also [25:12–14](https://ref.ly/Ezek25:12-Ezek25:14)). 

When Babylon destroyed Judah, Edom may have thought it had a chance to grow stronger. However, the Lord said that its success would not last long.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 35:1-15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Mount Seir (ID: `place:SeirMount`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Dispossess (ID: `keyterm:Dispossess`); Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); To Cause to Possess (ID: `keyterm:Possess`); Edom (ID: `place:Edom`)

## Ezekiel 35:5–10 (id: 11745)

The ancient hatred of Edom for Israel began with their ancestors, Esau and Jacob (see [Genesis 25:19–34](https://ref.ly/Gen25:19-Gen25:34); [27:1–46](https://ref.ly/Gen27:1-Gen27:46); [Numbers 20:14–21](https://ref.ly/Num20:14-Num20:21); [24:18](https://ref.ly/Num24:18); [2 Samuel 8:13–14](https://ref.ly/2Sam8:13-2Sam8:14); [1 Kings 11:14](https://ref.ly/1Kgs11:14)). Because of this hatred, the Edomites took advantage of Babylon’s destruction of Jerusalem. They attacked the Israelites when they were weak and helpless. They tried to destroy the descendants of Jacob and take the lands of Israel and Judah.

The Edomites enjoyed killing the Israelites. But the violence they brought on others would return to them. Their own people would die by the sword. God would punish their lasting hatred. Their land would become empty and ruined forever.

This prophecy came true in the 400s BC. During that time, a group of Arab tribes forced the Edomites out of their land.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 35:5-10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Dispossess (ID: `keyterm:Dispossess`); Mount Seir (ID: `place:SeirMount`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`)

## Ezekiel 35:13–15 (id: 11744)

The Edomites wrongly thought that God's judgment on his people and his leaving the temple meant his covenant with Israel had ended. The Edomites boasted and elevated themselves above both Israel and the Lord. 

The God of Israel would not allow such boasting, for he is the ruler of all. His choice of Israel and his gift of the land to them would not be taken back.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 35:13-15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Mount Seir (ID: `place:SeirMount`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); To Cause to Possess (ID: `keyterm:Possess`); Edom (ID: `place:Edom`)

The destruction of Edom (chapter [35](https://ref.ly/Ezek35:1-Ezek35:15)) would prepare the way for the restoration of the mountains of Israel*,* reversing the devastation threatened in chapter [6](https://ref.ly/Ezek6:1-Ezek6:14).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 36:1-15

## Ezekiel 36:2 (id: 11757)

Israel’s ancient high places (the hill country and mountains) could not be taken by their enemies. The Lord had given this land to his people.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 36:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ezekiel 36:6–7 (id: 11759)

The period of enduring the shame of mockery and plundering would now be over for Israel, and Israel’s enemies would soon endure their own shame by being mocked and plundered.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 36:6-7

## Ezekiel 36:8–11 (id: 11760)

When God’s people returned to their land, Israel would grow in number and in harvest. This would fulfill the command God gave at creation to “be fruitful and multiply” ([Genesis 1:28](https://ref.ly/Gen1:28)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 36:8-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ezekiel 36:12–13 (id: 11747)

God intended the land to provide plenty for his people and their children. Instead, it took their children and consumed its own people. This happened because Israel did not follow the covenant, leading to the Lord's judgment with disastrous effects for them and their children (see [Ezekiel 5:17](https://ref.ly/Ezek5:17)). Now that the people were changing, they would receive the covenant blessing of a fruitful land.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 36:12-13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Dispossess (ID: `keyterm:Dispossess`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); To Cause to Possess (ID: `keyterm:Possess`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Ezekiel 36:16–38 (id: 11748)

* Ezekiel reminded the people of their guilt and their need for God to change their hearts. In the future, God would cleanse his people.
* In the Old Testament, objects and people are divided into "clean" and "unclean," "sacred" and "profane" categories (see “Clean, Unclean, and Holy” Theme Note).

    God made Israel clean, but the Gentile nations remained unclean. Israel became unclean due to bloodshed and idolatry, which defiled the land. Because they acted like unclean nations, it was fitting for Israel to be scattered among them as punishment. In the future, God would make them clean so he could dwell among them again. Other nations, seeing his holiness in his people, would know he is the Holy One.

    In the New Testament, God’s redemption through Christ redefines the lines between clean and unclean (see [Acts 10:15](https://ref.ly/Acts10:15)). The Gentiles are no longer outside of God’s grace. They, too, can receive the Holy Spirit and become clean. Jews and Gentiles together now form the one people of God in Christ.

    Those in Christ Jesus are not only clean but also holy because of his priesthood. Therefore, they can come boldly into God’s presence and experience his grace ([Hebrews 12:18–29](https://ref.ly/Heb12:18-Heb12:29)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 36:16-38
* **Associated Articles:** Clean, Unclean, and Holy (ID: `500179@TyndaleStudyNotesThemes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`); Pure (ID: `keyterm:Pure`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Appointed Feast (ID: `keyterm:AppointedFeast`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Decree (ID: `keyterm:Decree`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Garden of Eden (ID: `place:Eden.2`); Grain (ID: `flora:Grain`)

## Ezekiel 36:17 (id: 11749)

* Covenant curses came to Israel because the people defiled their land through sinful behavior.
* A menstrual cloth became polluted by contact with a woman's monthly blood flow. This natural process was not sinful, but it made people ceremonially unclean. Any loss of bodily fluids, like blood, sweat (see study note on Ezekiel 44:17–19), or semen, also caused ceremonial uncleanness (see [Leviticus 15](https://ref.ly/Lev15:1-Lev15:33)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 36:17
* **Associated Articles:** Ezekiel 44:17-19 (ID: `11848@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Ezekiel 36:18 (id: 11750)

Israel made the land unsuitable for God's presence by committing murder and worshiping idols. Because they broke their covenant, they were expelled and scattered among the nations (see [Deuteronomy 29:22–28](https://ref.ly/Deut29:22-Deut29:28)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 36:18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`)

## Ezekiel 36:20 (id: 11751)

This scattering also brought shame to the Lord's holy name. It was not the exiles' behavior that took away the Lord's glory. Instead, their exile made other nations think Israel's God could not protect them in their own land.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 36:20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`)

## Ezekiel 36:21–24 (id: 11752)

God sent Israel into exile because he cared about his holiness. He wanted to protect the honor of his holy name, so he planned to bring them back to their land. Israel did not earn this return from exile; it showed the Lord's holiness and power to other nations. Israel could not stay outside the land God promised to Abraham and his descendants forever.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 36:21-24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Dishonor (ID: `keyterm:Dishonor`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`)

## Ezekiel 36:25 (id: 11753)

Bringing Israel back to the land was not enough. They would also become a new, transformed Israel. God would sprinkle them with clean water to cleanse them from all impurities that had defiled the land. Such sprinkling with water was a routine part of Jewish purification ceremonies (see [Numbers 19](https://ref.ly/Num19:1-Num19:22)). It symbolized a fresh start, with their old sins washed away.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 36:25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pure (ID: `keyterm:Pure`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`)

## Ezekiel 36:26 (id: 11754)

The Lord’s renewal of his people would not be only an outward cleansing. He would give Israel a new heart and a new spirit ([Ezekiel 11:19](https://ref.ly/Ezek11:19); [18:31](https://ref.ly/Ezek18:31)). 

In the Bible, the heart and spirit refer to a person’s inner thoughts and will, which guide actions. Their stubborn and resistant heart would become soft and responsive. They would be ready to serve the Lord.

Their spirit of rebellion would be replaced with a spirit of obedience.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 36:26


## Ezekiel 36:27–28 (id: 11755)

The spirit of God would bring life and light from darkness and chaos (see [Genesis 1:2](https://ref.ly/Gen1:2)). This would give people a new ability to follow God's rules and regulations. 

In the past, God's Spirit empowered individuals for specific tasks in service to the Lord (see [Judges 3:10](https://ref.ly/Judg3:10); [1 Samuel 16:13](https://ref.ly/1Sam16:13)). 

In the future, God's Spirit would empower more people to live holy lives (see [Joel 2:28–29](https://ref.ly/Joel2:28-Joel2:29)). These renewed people would live in Israel, making it suitable for God's presence to dwell among them again.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 36:27-28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Decree (ID: `keyterm:Decree`)

## Ezekiel 36:29–32 (id: 11756)

This change would bring the blessings of the covenant made with Moses, not its curses, and new honor among nearby nations. This blessing would make God's people deeply regret their past and recognize both their unworthiness and God's immense kindness.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 36:29-32

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Grain (ID: `flora:Grain`)

## Ezekiel 36:35–38 (id: 11758)

The restored land would become like the garden of Eden, a symbol of fertility and fruitfulness. The original garden would be enhanced by restored cities, filled with people like Jerusalem's streets during festivals. The greatest blessing would be God's willingness to hear Israel's prayers again. 

He had once refused to listen to his rebellious people ([Ezekiel 14:3](https://ref.ly/Ezek14:3); [20:3](https://ref.ly/Ezek20:3)), but now the Lord would turn his face toward them and hear their cries. The proof of this would be the number of people in the rebuilt cities who would acknowledge that the Lord is God.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 36:35-38

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Appointed Feast (ID: `keyterm:AppointedFeast`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Garden of Eden (ID: `place:Eden.2`)

## Ezekiel 37:1–14 (id: 11761)

The prophet saw a lively city full of life ([Ezekiel 36:38](https://ref.ly/Ezek36:38)). Then, he found himself in a valley filled with bones. This symbolized the promise that the spirit of the Lord gives life ([Ezekiel 36:16–38](https://ref.ly/Ezek36:16-Ezek36:38)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 37:1-14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Hope (ID: `keyterm:Hope`)

## Ezekiel 37:2 (id: 11768)

This death scene seemed hopeless. These were not recently dead bodies, but scattered bones, completely dried out. This scene symbolized the people's attitude. Their hopes were not just dead. Their hopes were broken and dried up.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 37:2


## Ezekiel 37:3 (id: 11769)

*S**on of man, can these bones come to life?*: The expected answer was no, but Ezekiel knew that God's power is unlimited. So, he asked God the question again. The real issue was not if the Lord could make these bones live, but if he wanted to do so.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 37:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ezekiel 37:4–6 (id: 11770)

* God wanted these bones to live. He communicated this through the message that Ezekiel was to deliver to the bones. Ezekiel declared they would become living, breathing bodies again, with flesh, muscles, and breath.
* The word translated as *breath* can also mean "spirit" or "wind," a wordplay that continues throughout this chapter.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 37:4-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`)

## Ezekiel 37:7–8 (id: 11771)

Ezekiel followed his task to prophesy to the bones, and they formed complete bodies. However, a body with bones, muscles, flesh, and skin is still lifeless. These people needed breath to live, as described in [Genesis 2:7](https://ref.ly/Gen2:7).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 37:7-8

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`)

## Ezekiel 37:9–10 (id: 11772)

When Ezekiel spoke to the four winds, breath entered the re\-formed bodies. They stood up as a great army ready for action. This breath, symbolizing being filled with the Spirit, gave them life and strength for action, just as it had happened to the prophet on two earlier occasions ([Ezekiel 1:28–2:2](https://ref.ly/Ezek1:28-Ezek2:2) and [3:23–24](https://ref.ly/Ezek3:23-Ezek3:24)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 37:9-10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ezekiel 37:11–14 (id: 11762)

The oracle that follows explains this vision. The people in exile felt as lifeless as old, dry bones. They believed all hope was lost. However, the Lord could and would restore them to life. God would call them his people again. He promised to open their graves of exile and bring them back to the land of Israel.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 37:11-14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Hope (ID: `keyterm:Hope`); Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`)

## Ezekiel 37:14 (id: 11763)

The Lord would give his life\-giving Spirit to his people. If the sovereign Lord decided to raise them, their dryness would not stop him.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 37:14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Ezekiel 37:15–28 (id: 11764)

The prophet then performed a sign action (see “Prophetic Sign Acts” Theme Note) that showed the future reunification of God's people and the healing of the division between the northern and southern tribes (see [1 Kings 12](https://ref.ly/1Kgs12:1-1Kgs12:33)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 37:15-28
* **Associated Articles:** Prophetic Sign Acts (ID: `500313@TyndaleStudyNotesThemes`)

## Ezekiel 37:19 (id: 11765)

The Lord, who has complete authority and power, would bring Israel back together by his own power.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 37:19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Tribe of Ephraim (ID: `group:Ephraim`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Joseph (ID: `person:Joseph.10`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Ephraim (ID: `person:Ephraim`)

## Ezekiel 37:20–25 (id: 11766)

When the kingdoms were joined together again, the problems that had caused the division would also be solved.

In the past, King Rehoboam’s harsh and unfaithful leadership divided the nation ([1 Kings 12](https://ref.ly/1Kgs12:1-1Kgs12:33)). Now God would give his people one servant leader, a shepherd king. Like David, this king would unite the tribes.

The restored people would also be made clean again. God would remove their idols and other sinful practices. The Lord would once again be their God.

After being made pure, they would obey the Lord’s laws and live in the land forever.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 37:20-25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); David (ID: `person:David`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Detested Thing (ID: `keyterm:DetestedThing`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Pure (ID: `keyterm:Pure`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Ezekiel 37:25–28 (id: 11767)

The covenant of peace is the blessing that comes from obeying the covenant. This covenant would last forever.

In the past, the people’s sin made them unclean. Because of this, the Lord allowed the temple to be destroyed. Now their new purity would be shown in a new sanctuary. God would live among them there forever.

This final temple would show the success of God’s work to make his people holy. It would demonstrate that the Lord is the one who makes Israel holy (see [Ezekiel 40–48](https://ref.ly/Ezek40:1-Ezek48:35)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 37:25-28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); David (ID: `person:David`); Peace (ID: `keyterm:IntactState`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`)

## Ezekiel 38:1–6 (id: 11774)

Gog would lead a group of seven nations from all over the world. The numbers seven and four symbolize completeness, showing an unbeatable alliance from every direction. Magog, Meshech, Tubal, Gomer, and Beth\-togarmah were in the north, in Anatolia and beyond the Black Sea. Persia was to the east, Ethiopia to the south, and Libya to the west.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 38:1-6

## Ezekiel 38:1–39.29 (id: 11773)

* In [Ezekiel 37](https://ref.ly/Ezek37:1-Ezek37:28), the nation is reunited and lives in peace in its own land. In [Ezekiel 38–39](https://ref.ly/Ezek38:1-Ezek39:29), this peaceful picture changes. A new danger appears. These chapters describe the attack, defeat, and removal of the final enemy, called *Gog*.

    Many readers have tried to identify Gog. Some have connected him with Gyges, king of Lydia, who ruled about 680 to 644 BC. However, the Gog described in the Bible goes beyond any one historical ruler. The text focuses less on who Gog is and more on the worldwide threat from the nations.

    Even this powerful enemy would not truly threaten God’s restored people. God was now living among them. Because of his presence, they were secure.

    These chapters use strong symbols. They are similar to psalms that speak about Zion’s safety (see, for example, [Psalms 2](https://ref.ly/Ps2:1-Ps2:12), [46](https://ref.ly/Ps46:1-Ps46:11)). The message is clear. If even a terrible enemy like Gog cannot defeat God’s people, then nothing in all creation can separate them from God’s protection.

* Ezekiel’s description of Gog also includes features of apocalyptic literature (a style of writing that uses symbols and dramatic images to describe God’s final victory over evil; see “Apocalyptic Literature” Theme Note).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 38:1-39:29
* **Associated Articles:** Apocalyptic Literature (ID: `500318@TyndaleStudyNotesThemes`)

## Ezekiel 38:4 (id: 11781)

Gog’s rebellion would remain fully under God’s control.

Gog and his army would think they were acting freely and attacking on their own. In reality, God would control their actions. They would be like prisoners led away with hooks in their jaws (compare[Ezekiel 29:4](https://ref.ly/Ezek29:4)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 38:4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Harpoon (ID: `realia:Harpoon`); Small Shield (ID: `realia:SmallShield`); Large Shield (ID: `realia:LargeShield`); Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`)

## Ezekiel 38:8 (id: 11782)

*After a long time* (literally *after many days*) *… In the latter years* (literally *in the end of the years*): This battle is described as a final and climactic conflict. It would come before a lasting time of peace.

Some people believe this battle will be a real event at the end of history. Others believe it is a symbolic picture (a literary image) that shows how the Lord protects his people in every age. In this view, the battle represents the ongoing struggle between God’s people and evil.

Whichever view is correct, the main message is clear. When the Lord’s favor rests on his people, nothing can remove them from his protection. Not even the strongest and most complete attack of evil can defeat them.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 38:8

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Safety (ID: `keyterm:Safety`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Ezekiel 38:9 (id: 11783)

*advancing like a thunderstorm*: This is similar to the threatening imagery in [Ezekiel 1](https://ref.ly/Ezek1:1-Ezek1:28), when God was Israel’s enemy. At that time, Israel sought protection from the rebellious nations around them. Now, Israel would seek protection from God against the nations.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 38:9

The Lord's "hooks" in Gog's "jaws" ([Ezekiel 38:4](https://ref.ly/Ezek38:4)) refers to Gog's evil plan to attack the defenseless Israelites and steal their goods (compare [Psalm 76:10](https://ref.ly/Ps76:10)).

Sheba and Dedan were in the east. Tarshish was in the west. Merchants from these distant places would come to buy and sell the goods taken in battle.

They believed that Gog and his allies would surely win. They expected to gain wealth from the spoils of war.

## Ezekiel 39:9–10 (id: 11794)

Israel would act only after Gog had been fully defeated and destroyed. Like some great battles in the past, the people would first watch the Lord fight for them. Then they would gather what was left behind (for example, [2 Kings 6:1–7:20](https://ref.ly/2Kgs6:1-2Kgs7:20); [2 Chronicles 20](https://ref.ly/2Chr20:1-2Chr20:37)). 

Fire from heaven would destroy Gog’s army. Only their wooden weapons would remain. Israel would use these weapons as firewood for seven years. In the Old Testament, the number seven often represents completeness.

Those who came to take goods from Israel would themselves be taken as goods. The attackers would lose everything. Their weapons would no longer be needed, because Israel’s final enemy had been destroyed.

## Ezekiel 39:18–20 (id: 11787)

In most sacrificial feasts, people eat animals that have been offered as sacrifices.

In this feast, the situation is reversed. Animals would eat the bodies of defeated enemies, as if they were rams, lambs, goats, and bulls.

This scene is the opposite of the great banquet described in [Isaiah 25:6](https://ref.ly/Isa25:6). In that future feast, God’s people are invited to celebrate. Here, God’s enemies become the meal instead of the guests.

## Ezekiel 40:1–48.35 (id: 11807)

The final section of the book of Ezekiel describes a new temple and a new division of the land.

First, Ezekiel describes the new temple ([Ezekiel 40:1–47:12](https://ref.ly/Ezek40:1-Ezek47:12); see “Temple Architecture as Theology” Theme Note; “Temple Legislation as Theology” Theme Note). This vision includes detailed plans for the building and its rules. It also shows how the temple’s design and laws teach about God.

Next, Ezekiel describes how the land will be divided among the tribes of Israel ([Ezekiel 47:13–48:35](https://ref.ly/Ezek47:13-Ezek48:35); see “Israel’s Geography as Theology” Theme Note). This section shows that even the geography (the way the land is arranged) has meaning.

The temple stands at the center of the land. This central place shows that God himself is at the center of his people. The temple is the final and most important part of God’s plan to restore (bring back and renew) and sanctify (make holy) his people. God promises that he will once again live among them (see [Ezekiel 37:28](https://ref.ly/Ezek37:28)). 

Ezekiel saw this temple in a vision. However, the people of Israel never built this exact temple in history.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:1-4
* **Associated Articles:** Temple Design as a Picture of God's Truth (ID: `500319@TyndaleStudyNotesThemes`); Temple Legislation as Theology (ID: `500320@TyndaleStudyNotesThemes`); Israel’s Geography as Theology (ID: `500321@TyndaleStudyNotesThemes`)

## Ezekiel 40:2 (id: 11813)

*a very high mountain*: In the Old Testament, God often revealed himself on mountaintops. Moses received God's law and the design for the tabernacle at Mount Sinai ([Exodus 19–40](https://ref.ly/Exod19:1-Exod40:38)). He also viewed the promised land, which he would never enter, from the top of Pisgah ([Deuteronomy 34:1–4](https://ref.ly/Deut34:1-Deut34:4)). Similarly, on this mountaintop, Ezekiel met with God and saw the wonderful future God had prepared for his people, even though he would not live to experience it.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Vision (ID: `keyterm:Vision`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Ezekiel 40:3 (id: 11816)

*a man whose appearance was like bronze*: An angelic guide would lead Ezekiel's tour of the temple. The bronze color reminds us of the heavenly creatures described in the first vision (see [Ezekiel 1:7](https://ref.ly/Ezek1:7)). 

Unlike the guide for his earlier vision of the earthly temple in [Ezekiel 8–11](https://ref.ly/Ezek8:1-Ezek11:25), this guide carried only a linen measuring cord and a measuring rod. These are tools for building, not destroying. 

Measurement is a key theme in the following chapters. It allows the prophet to emphasize the importance of certain parts of the temple by making them larger and more precisely defined than other parts.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Measuring Reed (ID: `realia:MeasuringReed`); Reed (ID: `realia:Reed`); Thread (ID: `realia:Thread`); Linen (ID: `realia:Linen`); Reed (ID: `flora:Reed`)

## Ezekiel 40:5 (id: 11823)

*a wall surrounding the temple area*: Walls create and define space, marking an "inside" and an "outside." This wall was important. Its function was to separate the "profane" \[unholy] area outside the temple from the holy area inside. This ensured the important distinction would never again be forgotten (see [Ezekiel 22:26](https://ref.ly/Ezek22:26)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cubit (ID: `realia:Cubit`); Reed (ID: `realia:Reed`); Handbreadth (ID: `realia:Handbreadth`); Measuring Reed (ID: `realia:MeasuringReed`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Reed (ID: `flora:Reed`)

## Ezekiel 40:5–16 (id: 11822)

The exact architectural details of the temple are difficult to translate. Some of the building terms are hard to understand in every language. However, the overall impression of these gates is clear.

The gates looked like fortresses (strong buildings used for defense). They were built to keep out people who did not have permission to enter. These gates protected the holy space of the temple.

The eastern gateway is described first because it was the most important. It stood on the sacred east–west line (a straight line from east to west) on which the whole temple was built. This gateway is also important because the glory of the Lord (God’s visible and powerful presence) would return through it ([Ezekiel 43:1–5](https://ref.ly/Ezek43:1-Ezek43:5)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:5-16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cubit (ID: `realia:Cubit`); Reed (ID: `realia:Reed`); Measuring Reed (ID: `realia:MeasuringReed`); Entrance (ID: `realia:Entrance`); Reed (ID: `flora:Reed`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Doorstep (ID: `realia:Doorstep`); Handbreadth (ID: `realia:Handbreadth`); Date Palm (ID: `flora:DatePalm`); Window (ID: `realia:Window`)

## Ezekiel 40:7 (id: 11824)

Six guard alcoves (small rooms for guards) lined the inside of each gate. There were three on each side. This design shows that the gates had a defensive purpose.

The layout of these gates was similar to gates found at Hazor, Megiddo, and Gezer. Archaeologists have uncovered gates like these in those cities. However, the temple gates in Ezekiel’s vision were much larger than the ones found there.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Measuring Reed (ID: `realia:MeasuringReed`); Doorstep (ID: `realia:Doorstep`); Cubit (ID: `realia:Cubit`); Reed (ID: `realia:Reed`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Reed (ID: `flora:Reed`)

## Ezekiel 40:16 (id: 11808)

The carved palm trees remind readers of the rich plant life of the garden of Eden. Palm trees were a sign of life and fruitfulness (that is, the ability to grow and produce food). Similar carvings appear in the temple described in [1 Kings 6:29](https://ref.ly/1Kgs6:29).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Window (ID: `realia:Window`); Date Palm (ID: `flora:DatePalm`); Entrance (ID: `realia:Entrance`)

## Ezekiel 40:17–19 (id: 11809)

The outer courtyard formed a buffer zone (a space that protects another space) around the holy things in the inner courtyard. This open area separated common space from the most sacred space. In this way, it helped guard what was holy.

Thirty rooms were built along the walls of the outer courtyard. The text does not clearly state the purpose of these rooms. It also does not give their exact measurements. This lack of detail increases the contrast between the less important outer area and the central holy space, which receives much more attention.

These rooms were most likely used by the Levites. The Levites were members of the tribe of Levi who served in temple worship. They probably used these rooms for different tasks related to their service.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:17-19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cubit (ID: `realia:Cubit`)

## Ezekiel 40:20–27 (id: 11810)

The north gateway and the south gateway are described in similar ways to the east gateway. However, the description gives fewer details than it does for the east gateway. Even so, these gates were strong and impressive structures.

Like the east gateway, they served as a powerful defensive barrier. They were meant to prevent any defilement (anything unclean or impure) from entering the sacred area.

There was no west gateway to either the outer court or the inner court. The area behind the main temple building was closed off. This design prevented anyone from entering the temple area from the rear.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:20-27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cubit (ID: `realia:Cubit`); Entrance (ID: `realia:Entrance`); Window (ID: `realia:Window`); Date Palm (ID: `flora:DatePalm`)

## Ezekiel 40:22 (id: 11811)

Steps led up to each gate. These steps made the gates feel less accessible. A person had to go up in order to enter the sacred area.

The inner parts of the temple were higher than the outer parts. The more sacred the space, the higher it stood. This difference in height created another level of separation between common space and holy space.

In this way, the design of the temple showed that not every place was the same. Some areas were set apart as more sacred than others.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Window (ID: `realia:Window`); Date Palm (ID: `flora:DatePalm`)

## Ezekiel 40:28–34 (id: 11812)

The inner courtyard was divided from the outer courtyard by another set of large gateways. These gateways were similar in size and purpose to those of the outer courtyard. The entry rooms of these gateways faced outward toward the outer courtyard, unlike the outer gates, where they faced inward.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:28-34

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Entrance (ID: `realia:Entrance`); Cubit (ID: `realia:Cubit`); Window (ID: `realia:Window`); Date Palm (ID: `flora:DatePalm`)

## Ezekiel 40:35–37 (id: 11814)

The text does not mention a wall around the inner courtyard. This may be because the inner court stood higher than the outer court. It was raised by another eight steps, which may have been about 2\.4 meters (about 8 feet) high.

If there were no wall, there may have been a free\-standing archway (an open structure shaped like an arch). This design would allow people to see what was happening inside. At the same time, the height would prevent anyone from stepping into the sacred area by accident.

Another possibility is that the text simply does not mention the wall. The writer may have left out that detail, even if a wall was present.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:35-37

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Entrance (ID: `realia:Entrance`); Window (ID: `realia:Window`); Date Palm (ID: `flora:DatePalm`); Cubit (ID: `realia:Cubit`)

## Ezekiel 40:38–43 (id: 11815)

The sacrificial animals were killed and prepared in rooms beside the gateways that led into the inner courtyard. This detail shows the main purpose of the new temple. It was a place for sacrifice.

The animals had to be washed and cut into pieces before the priests offered them on the altar. These steps prepared the animals for worship.

In Ezekiel’s vision, this new temple strongly focuses on sacrifices that atoned for sin. These sacrifices dealt with sin and restored the relationship between God and humanity that sin had broken. By contrast, the temple in Jerusalem was both a place for sacrifice and a house of prayer (see [1 Kings 8:27–30](https://ref.ly/1Kgs8:27-1Kgs8:30), [52–53](https://ref.ly/1Kgs8:52-1Kgs8:53); [Isaiah 56:6–8](https://ref.ly/Isa56:6-Isa56:8); [Matthew 21:13](https://ref.ly/Matt21:13)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:38-43

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); Slaughtering (ID: `keyterm:Slaughtering`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Entrance (ID: `realia:Entrance`); Handbreadth (ID: `realia:Handbreadth`); Hewn Stone (ID: `realia:HewnStone`); Cubit (ID: `realia:Cubit`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

The hooks might be used to store the knives and tools mentioned in [Ezekiel 40:42](https://ref.ly/Ezek40:42).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:43

## Ezekiel 40:46 (id: 11818)

The Lord finally rewarded Zadok's loyalty (see [Ezekiel 44:15](https://ref.ly/Ezek44:15)). He decreed that only Zadok's descendants could approach the Lord to serve him. Only those chosen by the Lord and fit to enter the holy space could access God in this temple.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:46

## Ezekiel 40:47 (id: 11819)

The themes of sacrifice and limited access come together in the description of the inner courtyard's size. It was a perfect square, a common shape for holy spaces in the Old Testament (see [Exodus 26:1–35](https://ref.ly/Exod26:1-Exod26:35); [1 Kings 6:20](https://ref.ly/1Kgs6:20)), measuring 175 feet on each side. At its center stood the altar of sacrifice, the only piece of furniture there. People had to offer holy sacrifices without risking impurity to ensure the Lord's presence when he returned to fill the temple with his glory.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:47

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Altar (ID: `realia:Altar`); Temple Altar (ID: `realia:TempleAltar`); Stone Altar (ID: `realia:StoneAltar`); Cubit (ID: `realia:Cubit`); Tabernacle Altar (ID: `realia:TabernacleAltar`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Ezekiel 40:48–41.3 (id: 11820)

The temple stood at the protected center of the temple complex. It was next to the inner court. This location showed its importance.

The temple also stood at the highest point in the complex. It was ten steps higher than the inner court. The inner court itself was eight steps higher than the outer court. This rising height showed increasing holiness as a person moved inward.

Like Solomon’s temple before it, this temple had three main areas. These were:

* the entry room,
* the sanctuary (the main holy room), and
* the Most Holy Place (the most sacred room).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:48-41:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cubit (ID: `realia:Cubit`); Entrance (ID: `realia:Entrance`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Ezekiel 40:49 (id: 11821)

Outside the entry room were square columns with an unknown purpose, similar to those in Solomon's temple ([1 Kings 7:15–22](https://ref.ly/1Kgs7:15-1Kgs7:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 40:49

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cubit (ID: `realia:Cubit`); Entrance (ID: `realia:Entrance`)

## Ezekiel 41:1–2 (id: 11825)

The temple walls were about 3\.2 meters thick (10\.5 feet). These thick walls showed strength and protection. At this point, the gates were replaced by a doorway. Only the priests were allowed to enter the inner court around the temple, so a heavy gate was not needed.

The sanctuary was the most important room in the new temple. For this reason, the vision describes it in greater detail than the other rooms. It also gives more exact measurements for this space.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 41:1-2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cubit (ID: `realia:Cubit`); Entrance (ID: `realia:Entrance`)

## Ezekiel 41:3–4 (id: 11828)

The inner room was the Most Holy Place. It was the only square room in the temple. This room was the most sacred space in the building.

A person reached it by passing through three openings. Each opening was narrower than the one before it. Access became more limited as a person moved closer to God.

The entrance to the entry room was about 7\.5 meters wide (24\.5 feet; [Ezekiel 40:48](https://ref.ly/Ezek40:48)). The doorway into the sanctuary was about 5\.3 meters wide (17\.5 feet; [41:2](https://ref.ly/Ezek41:2)). The entrance to the Most Holy Place was only about 3\.2 meters wide (10\.5 feet).

Ezekiel did not enter the Most Holy Place. He stood outside and waited while the angel went in alone and measured the room.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 41:3-4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cubit (ID: `realia:Cubit`); Most Holy Place (ID: `place:MostHolyPlace`); Entrance (ID: `realia:Entrance`)

## Ezekiel 41:5–26 (id: 11829)

The temple had 90 side rooms on three levels. Behind it was a large building with an unknown purpose, possibly to prevent unauthorized access. No one could approach God's presence from behind. The side rooms might have stored priestly clothing and equipment.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 41:5-26

## Ezekiel 41:15–20 (id: 11826)

The temple building was covered with wooden panels. The walls were decorated with carvings of palm trees and cherubim.

The cherubim were like those Ezekiel saw in his earlier visions of judgment (see [Ezekiel 1:5–12](https://ref.ly/Ezek1:5-Ezek1:12); [10:2–14](https://ref.ly/Ezek10:2-Ezek10:14)). In those visions, the cherubim had four faces. However, the carved images in the temple had only two faces. One face was a lion, the greatest of the wild animals. The other was a human, the highest part of God’s created order.

Cherubim also decorated Solomon’s temple ([1 Kings 6:32](https://ref.ly/1Kgs6:32)). Cherubim were traditional symbols of judgment. The palm trees were traditional symbols of blessing. Together, these images showed both God’s judgment and his blessing.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 41:15-20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Date Palm (ID: `flora:DatePalm`); Winged Creature (ID: `realia:WingedCreature`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Doorstep (ID: `realia:Doorstep`); Cubit (ID: `realia:Cubit`); Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`); Window (ID: `realia:Window`)

## Ezekiel 41:22 (id: 11827)

The only piece of furniture was a wooden altar, the table that stands in the Lord’s presence. This is likely where the priests daily placed the bread of the presence before the Lord ([Exodus 25:30](https://ref.ly/Exod25:30)). Describing this table as an altar emphasizes the focus on sacrifice in Ezekiel’s temple.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 41:22

Ezekiel reached the center of the temple complex and then started moving outward again.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 42:1


## Ezekiel 42:3–12 (id: 11833)

The priests' rooms were on three levels at the back of the sanctuary. Priests entered from the top level in the inner court ([Ezekiel 42:12](https://ref.ly/Ezek42:12)) and exited at the bottom in the outer court ([42:9](https://ref.ly/Ezek42:9)). These rooms served as transition spaces for the priests' duties as they moved between the inner and outer courts.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 42:3-12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cubit (ID: `realia:Cubit`)

## Ezekiel 42:13–14 (id: 11830)

The priests stored and ate the most sacred offerings in these rooms. They also kept their holy clothes there. When not serving in the Lord's presence, the priests changed into other clothes because the holy clothes were special. These rules show more care in separating the holy from the ordinary compared to similar laws in Leviticus (see [Leviticus 6](https://ref.ly/Lev6:1-Lev6:30)).

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 42:13-14

## Ezekiel 42:16–20 (id: 11831)

After finishing his tour of the inner courtyard, Ezekiel saw the overall dimensions of the area. The entire complex was square, symbolizing holiness. This shape set it apart from Solomon's Temple and the tabernacle, where only the Most Holy Place was square. The description of the temple ends where it started, mentioning a wall surrounding it (see [Ezekiel 40:5](https://ref.ly/Ezek40:5)). Ezekiel reminds us that the wall's purpose was to separate the holy from the common.

* **Associated Passages:** Ezekiel 42:16-20
